Sea Of Stars Is Getting A 'Sunset Edition' Switch 2 Physical And A Gorgeous Final Merch Drop

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Sabotage Studio's Sea of Stars is no stranger to a physical release or merch drop, and today, iam8bit has announced that it has one final collection in store.

A brand new Switch 2 physical, the Sea of Stars: Sunset Edition ($49.99), is the star of the show, launching on 2nd April 2027. This one packs the full game and all of its expansions on the cartridge (what a concept!), and comes with some sweet sticker sheets and a download code for the game's soundtrack.

There's a beefier option in the Exclusive Edition, which also boasts an exclusive sleeve and an in-box manual, for those after a little throwback.

The beefiest of the bunch, mind you, is still to come. The Collector's Edition contains all of the goodies outlined above, and throws in a bunch of extra merch too. We're talking a double-sided poster, a tote bag, a reversible bandana, a Rainbow Conch keychain, and seven pins, all housed in a Rainbow Conch Chest. Come on, that's sweet.

The Collector's Edition comes in at a pricey $129.99 (or you can just get the merch for $79.99). All of the above are now available to pre-order from the iam8bit website until 11th January, before shipping in April.

As if all of that wasn't enough, iam8bit has also announced that it has two vinyl LPs in the pipeline, one all about the main game soundtrack, and one featuring the tunes from the Throes of the Watchmaker DLC. You'll also find pre-order information about these on the iam8bit site.

Trezor Warns 27,000 Shipping Orders Were Exposed in Partner Breach, Not Wallet Keys

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Strict editorial policy that focuses on accuracy, relevance, and impartiality

Morbi pretium leo et nisl aliquam mollis. Quisque arcu lorem, ultricies quis pellentesque nec, ullamcorper eu odio.

Hardware-wallet maker Trezor has disclosed a breach at shipping partner Skladon affecting data connected with approximately 27,000 customer orders. The incident is serious because a delivery address and product purchase history can reveal who may own crypto. It is also important not to turn a data-exposure story into a false claim that customer wallets were drained. Trezor says its own systems were not compromised, and the disclosed records did not include wallet backups, PINs or passphrases.

Trezor says Skladon informed it on November 15 of unauthorized access linked to an incident on November 7. The data at issue concerned orders placed between August 9 and November 7, 2026. The company estimates roughly 27,000 affected orders and says the investigation is continuing. The records included names, phone numbers, delivery addresses, email addresses and information about orders and products bought. A limited number of earlier August customers may also have been affected even though their information had been anonymized within Trezor’s own systems before the notification process.

Someone with a buyer’s name, address and exact device order can craft a convincing phishing message or impersonate customer support. The same combination may create physical-security concerns if a criminal believes the household controls valuable assets. None of this requires compromising the hardware wallet’s cryptographic security. Trezor says it never requests wallet recovery data by telephone and warns affected users to treat unexpected calls, texts, emails and letters with suspicion. If a message uses authentic order details, that does not make its instructions legitimate.

For related context, see our earlier report on the Unocoin wallet-key incident.

The company says it has contacted affected customers it can still reach and notified a data-protection authority. It is introducing or expanding anonymous pickup at collection points in much of Europe, reducing the amount of residential delivery information involved in future orders. Trezor also says it is working to shorten customer-data retention from 90 to 45 days, with further reductions under consideration. Those steps address the long-term exposure of operational records rather than claiming that a damaged secret key can be repaired after the fact.

Customers should go directly to the vendor’s known website for official support rather than follow contact details supplied in an unsolicited message. They should never type a recovery phrase into a web form or share it with a caller. Buyers can also consider whether their delivery address and household security require extra attention. Anyone receiving threats should contact appropriate local authorities. The precise number of affected people, rather than orders, may differ, and the final breach scope may change as investigators establish what was exported.

A legitimate package or delivery notice is especially convincing because it can refer to a real order. Criminals might use the information for emails, texts, calls or fraudulent web pages that look like shipping support. Wallet owners should be wary of any message that asks them to type a recovery phrase, install a replacement wallet application or approve an unexpected transaction. Those requests are unrelated to normal delivery updates. The strongest precaution is to verify a message through a separately opened official website, not by following a link in the message itself.

For related context, see our earlier report on Coinbase's discussion of cold-storage controls.

The distinction is straightforward: a shipping-partner breach can expose sensitive personal information without exposing the keys securing funds. For users, that does not make the incident harmless; it shifts the immediate risk toward targeted impersonation and privacy rather than a protocol-level wallet failure.

Reporting sources: Trezor official incident report

My Green Condo Inc. Opens Patented Blockchain Accounting Framework (U.S. Patent No. 12,443,952) to SaaS Providers for M&A and Strategic Licensing

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Building on its 2026 blockchain and tokenization roadmap, MGCOne invites legacy property management software vendors to eliminate management-driven churn and acquire a defensible IP moat.

HIGHTSTOWN, N.J., Nov. 17, 2026 /PRNewswire/ -- My Green Condo Inc. (MGCOne), the pioneer in association-centric PropTech, today announced it is formalizing strategic licensing, joint venture, and M&A discussions with established accounting and property management SaaS providers to commercialize its foundational intellectual property, U.S. Patent No. 12,443,952.

Following its September industry brief on tokenized real estate, stablecoin settlements, and AI orchestration, My Green Condo Inc. is opening its patented blockchain accounting architecture to incumbent software vendors looking to transition away from aging, vendor-centric database models.

Solving the SaaS Churn and Silo Problem In the traditional community management ecosystem, accounting and ERP software is sold to management companies rather than the Condo, HOA, and Co-op associations themselves. When an association switches management firms, the incumbent accounting provider routinely loses the account, creating structural revenue volatility and costly data migrations.

"Legacy SaaS platforms were built around intermediaries, trapping communities in fragmented databases and leaving software providers exposed to constant contract churn," said Savitha Sathyan, Co-Founder and Head of Technology at My Green Condo Inc. "With our patent, we hold the protected architectural blueprint for on-chain association accounting and instant estoppel verification. Rather than forcing incumbents to spend years on foundational R&D, we are offering established SaaS leaders an immediate path to license or acquire this IP and lead the industry's next era."

Strategic Advantages for SaaS Acquirers and Licensees:

My Green Condo Inc. is actively engaging corporate development teams and software executives to structure bilateral licensing agreements or strategic acquisitions.

My Green Condo Inc. is a PropTech company delivering end-to-end community management solutions for Condo, HOA, and Co-op associations. Founded by former Wall Street bankers and seasoned association board members, the company is dedicated to modernizing property management.

The company's flagship platform, MGCOne, is the industry's first patented, association-centered operating system. Engineered to elevate community living and modernize governance, MGCOne empowers property managers, board members, and residents through advanced automation and seamless communication. The platform grants communities' absolute control over their data, financial ledgers, and operational continuity.

Leveraging advanced blockchain infrastructure and next-generation AI, MGCOne centralizes operations into an ultra-secure "System of Record." Inspired by the Amazon marketplace model, it delivers unprecedented transparency, robust security, and independence for residents, board members, and service providers alike.

For more information, visit www.mygreencondo.net.

NPM Workspaces: Why Does My Monorepo Build Work Locally but Break in CI?

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

It's 6:40 pm. Your pull request is green on your laptop. You push, CI goes red, and the error says Cannot find module '@acme/core'. You didn't touch that import. Nobody did. You re-run the pipeline anyway, because hope is a strategy, and it fails again.

If you've got two or three packages sharing code in one repo, you've probably met some version of this. NPM workspaces are the built-in way to keep those packages consistent: one install, one lockfile, shared dependency versions, and one command that runs a script everywhere. But the feature has sharp edges that most tutorials skip.

Here's the open question for this article: why did the build pass on your machine and fail on a clean checkout? We'll answer it before the end.

If you like practical monorepo write-ups like this, follow me here on Medium. I post one every week.

By the end you'll know how to set up workspaces, run scripts recursively, filter to the packages you care about, fake parallel runs (npm doesn't do them natively), and avoid the hoisting trap that hides missing dependencies.

Workspaces let one root package.json manage several packages in the same repository. They landed in npm 7, so any modern Node install has them. Check yours:

Before you rely on any flag in this article, skim the official npm workspaces docs for your version. The CLI is still moving.

Before we get into the examples, a quick note: The code snippets provided here are meant purely for understanding the concept. Always refer to the official documentation for the most current API and syntax.

We'll build one small repo and grow it as we go. Two packages: @acme/core (a tiny shared library) and @acme/app (a consumer). Both are TypeScript.

(Strip the // comment line if you paste these into real JSON files. I'm using it as the file name label.)

Two things matter here. "private": true stops you from accidentally publishing the root. And TypeScript is installed once, at the root, so every package uses the same compiler version. That's the "make things the same" part: one version of the tool, everywhere.

Those are symlinks. Your workspace packages show up in the root node_modules as links to the real folders, so import "@acme/core" resolves to your local source instead of the registry. Edit core, and the app sees it. No npm link dance.

Real-world version. Notice the "*" range for @acme/core. npm sees that a local workspace satisfies it and links the folder instead of fetching anything. If you pin a version the workspace doesn't match, say ^2.0.0 while core is at 1.0.0, npm will go to the registry and install a copy from there. That's a quiet source of "why is my local change ignored" bugs.

Under the hood. There's a single package-lock.json at the root. Workspaces are treated as link nodes in one dependency tree, which npm's tree builder (Arborist) dedupes and hoists to the root node_modules where it can. One tree, one lockfile, one answer to "which version of this is installed?"

So far everything looks smooth. What happens when you run the build for the whole repo?

The answer to "run this script in every package" is the --workspaces flag, or -ws for short.

npm walks each workspace and runs its build script. If some packages lack the script, add --if-present so npm skips them instead of failing:

Look at that order. App built before core. Keep that in mind.

Here's the trap. On your laptop, you already built core yesterday, so packages/core/dist exists. App's tsc finds the old dist/index.d.ts, compiles happily, and the whole thing looks fine.

On a clean CI checkout, dist doesn't exist. App builds first (workspaces from a glob like packages/* typically resolve alphabetically, and app comes before core), and TypeScript can't find the types:

That's the answer to our open question. The recursive flag runs workspaces in the order npm lists them. It does not read your dependency graph. Locally, a stale dist folder was covering for the missing ordering.

The fix is to be explicit about order in the root script:

Now clean checkouts and warm laptops behave the same. To prove it to yourself, delete both dist folders and run npm run build from the root. It should succeed every time.

Have you ever had a build that only worked because of leftover output on your disk? Hold that thought, there's a cousin of it later in the article.

Under the hood. --workspaces is a config switch that tells the command to run in the context of all configured workspaces. Commands that touch the dependency tree (install, update) link workspaces into node_modules. Commands that run things (test, exec, publish) go to the root project unless you pass a workspace flag. That's why a bare npm test at the root doesn't run your packages' tests. It runs the root's.

The root script solved ordering, but typing -w @acme/core all day gets old. Is there a better way to aim at one package?

NPM doesn't have a separate "filter" command. The -w (or --workspace) flag is the filter.

All three target the same package. The docs say valid values are a workspace name, a path to a workspace directory, or a path to a parent directory, which selects every workspace inside it. The short folder name works in practice too, but I'd use the package name in scripts because it survives folder renames.

Adding dependencies is where -w earns its keep:

The dependency lands in core's package.json, and the root lockfile updates. You never hand-edit the wrong file again.

Want to include the root project in a workspace run? Use --include-workspace-root:

And the escape hatch: --workspaces=false tells npm to ignore workspaces for a command, which is handy when a config file sets them on by default.

For smarter filtering, use npm query. It takes CSS-like selectors:

Both print a JSON array of matching packages. It's a good way to feed names into a script, though I'd keep it for CI tooling and not daily use.

Under the hood. -w doesn't change which tree gets built. The whole repo still has one dependency tree. The flag only narrows which packages the command runs against. That's also why installing with -w can still touch the shared lockfile and hoisted node_modules.

Here's a quick mid-article check: do you target workspaces by name or by folder in your own scripts? Tell me which and why in the comments. I'm curious whether folder paths are still winning out there.

Filtering gets one package. What about running several at the same time?

Not by themselves. --workspaces goes one at a time. Anyone claiming otherwise is describing a different tool.

For long-running tasks like watch mode, add concurrently at the root:

Run npm run dev and you'll see interleaved, labeled output like:

Parallel is for independent work, like watchers and linters. Do not run builds in parallel when one package consumes another's output. That's the build-order bug again, but now it's flaky instead of consistent, which is worse. Flaky bugs eat evenings.

My rule: sequential && for builds, parallel for watchers.

Under the hood. concurrently just spawns child processes and prefixes their output. Each npm run ... -w call is its own npm process doing its own tree lookup. That's fine for a handful of packages. If you find yourself orchestrating dozens with caching and a task graph, that's the point to look at a dedicated build tool, such as Turborepo or Nx, and keep npm workspaces underneath for installs. Workspaces handle installs and linking. They aren't a task runner, and that's okay.

Next up is the trap I teased earlier, the sibling of the stale dist folder.

Because npm hoists shared dependencies to the root node_modules, a package can import something it never declared and still work.

Say core uses dayjs and declares it. App imports it too, but forgot to add it to its own package.json:

It works, because dayjs sits in the root node_modules where app's Node resolution finds it. The bug is invisible until someone publishes @acme/app on its own, or moves it to another repo, and it explodes with "module not found."

The fix is boring and correct: declare what you import.

Now both packages list dayjs themselves. npm still stores one copy at the root if the versions agree, so you keep the disk savings and the honesty.

My opinion: this is the number one thing most developers get wrong with workspaces. They treat the root node_modules like a shared bucket. It's a cache of the tree, not a permission slip.

Under the hood. Hoisting is a dedupe optimization in the tree builder, and Node's resolution walks up parent folders looking for node_modules. The two together create "phantom dependencies." Stricter package managers fight this by design. With npm you have to enforce the discipline yourself, for example through a lint rule or a quick review habit.

Last stretch. Workspaces have more commands that keep your packages consistent with each other.

Most npm commands accept -w or -ws. Four are worth knowing.

Scaffold a new package. npm init -w creates the folder if it doesn't exist and adds it to the root workspaces setup:

Edit package.json fields everywhere. This is my favorite consistency tool:

The first sets the same Node engine range in every package. The second prints each package's version, keyed by name.

Here's the full running example one more time, the root file you should end up with:

I kept typecheck out of the earlier sections on purpose. Try it, and think about why --noEmit against app would fail on a clean checkout (hint: same cause as before, no core types without a core build). That's your homework.

Remember 6:40 pm and the missing @acme/core? Your laptop had a stale dist folder quietly doing the job that build ordering should have done. CI had no such luxury. Workspaces gave you linking, one lockfile, and one install. They did not give you a dependency-aware build order, and they never promised to.

Further reading from folks who've covered this ground: Faiz Ahmed on npm workspaces, a gentle intro from Wasp, Safeguard's dependency walkthrough, the Publishing Project's take, and Paul Serban's guide. If a teammate keeps fighting this exact monorepo build bug, send them this article.

Did this approach match how you are solving it, or do you have a different take? Drop a comment. I genuinely read every single one.

If this saved you even a few minutes of debugging or confusion, hit that clap button so others can find it too. It really does make a difference.

Branch isolation in a live Supabase app

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 17 • Originally published at syedmuhammadhussain.pages.dev

To keep branches apart in one Supabase database, give every branch-owned table a region_id, fill it in for existing rows, make it NOT NULL, stamp it on the server, and let Row Level Security decide which rows each signed-in user gets back. Filtering in React isn't enough, because the browser holds a public key and can ask the database for anything. Check the result with row counts, because RLS hides rows without raising an error.

I built Zarrar Cooling Services (ZCS) on my own: a React and Supabase platform for an appliance repair and cooling business, with a public booking site, an admin panel, a technician panel, and branch views. Version 1 served one branch, Islamabad (which also covers Rawalpindi). Then the business opened a branch in Jhelum.

The requirement sounded simple. Staff in one branch must not see another branch's jobs, payroll, invoices, or stock. The owner must see everything. And the app was live, with real bookings coming in, so I couldn't rebuild it from an empty database.

Most Supabase guides start from an empty database. This one starts from 24 tables of real data: the model I chose, the migration in the order I ran it, the policies, and what I have and haven't tested.

So anyone can copy the publishable key from DevTools and query the Data API directly. A region dropdown in React is a convenience; only a policy in Postgres keeps Jhelum's payroll away from an Islamabad technician. In ZCS the /admin routes only checked that you were signed in, so RLS was already the real authorisation layer.

This isn't a theoretical risk. UpGuard found 16,326 Supabase databases with publicly readable tables among about 300,000 domains showing signs of Supabase use, and more than half had indicators of personal data (UpGuard, "Everything, everywhere: systemic data exposure in Supabase apps"; the page is dated 14 Oct 2026, and BleepingComputer covered it on 28 Sep 2026). BleepingComputer summarises the causes as "missing or ineffective row-level security policies and misuse of public keys." OWASP ranks broken access control first in its 2025 Top 10 and says access control "is only effective when implemented in trusted server-side code" (OWASP A01:2025).

A tenant is a separate customer organisation sharing your infrastructure. Tenants must never see each other's data, and nobody on the tenant side sees across tenants. ZCS isn't that. It's one company with several branches, which I'll call regions because that's the name in the schema.

Multi-tenant SaaS compared with ZCS regions

What carries over is the mechanism: an ID column on every owned row, a table linking users to the IDs they may access, a SECURITY DEFINER helper for that lookup, and policies that use it. For real multi-tenancy the migration steps below still apply, but the trust model usually needs to be stricter, for example no cross-tenant super-users inside the app.

Before writing any SQL, I compared five options:

The deciding argument was "existing hooks keep working": my plan estimated that about 14 pages would need no code changes, because the same select simply returns fewer rows.

Regions are data, not an enum, so a new branch never needs a migration:

24 tables are region-owned and get a region_id: work orders, invoices, expenses, staff, inventory, suppliers, purchase orders, assets, the payroll tables, notes, reports, activity logs, and invoice settings. Child tables such as attachments and invoice items take the region of their parent. Shared tables (services, system settings) stay global. Customers are a special case: the table has no region_id, and any branch can add to it, but a policy only shows a customer to a branch that has a work order for them.

One function answers "may the current user touch this region?":

Real ZCS helper (migration 20260906101730)

It's SECURITY DEFINER (it runs with its owner's rights) so it can read user_regions and super_admins without triggering their own RLS. A policy that queries another RLS-protected table can recurse into itself. ZCS had already been bitten by that on user_roles and fixed it with the same pattern, which is the standard answer in the Supabase community.

Figure: a request carries the user's JWT; Postgres runs the policy, which asks the helper whether this user may access the row's region. For super-admins, the region switcher narrows what the database returns, because the helper reads user_active_region. That's a scope they choose, not a security boundary. For everyone else, it's only a badge.

I never migrated the database the website was using. I made a copy of it as a new Supabase project and ran every migration below on that copy. I tested the updated app against it on my computer, and ran the same changes on a local Supabase stack.

When everything passed, I put the site into maintenance mode during off-hours, so nothing new was being written. I copied across the data the live database had gained since the copy was made, then changed the production environment variables so the website points to the new project. The original database is untouched and kept as a backup.

The site was in maintenance mode during the switch, and I didn't time it, so I'm not claiming zero downtime.

The first step wasn't SQL that changed anything. I counted every row in the 24 region-owned tables as the postgres role, which bypasses RLS: a little under 300 rows in total, plus the customers and auth users. It's a small business; the method matters more than the size. Every later phase had to leave those numbers unchanged. Under RLS, a denied read returns an empty result with no error, so a broken policy looks exactly like an empty table, and "the page loads" proves nothing.

All of this ran on 6 September 2026. Each phase was its own migration file, created with supabase migration new, applied through the SQL editor, and written with a ROLLBACK block in its header.

The migration phases, all run on 6 September 2026

Adding a nullable column with no default doesn't rewrite the table in PostgreSQL (PostgreSQL: ALTER TABLE). It still takes a short exclusive lock, which is harmless at this size and something to plan for on large tables.

The backfill put every row in Islamabad, even jobs that were really Jhelum work; those moved later, one at a time, through a "Move to region" action. A uniform backfill kept every check to one question: is the total still the same?

Phase 3, abridged (migration 20260906120956).

Phase 5 is where the column became enforced. The trigger matters as much as the constraint:

Phase 5, abridged (migration 20260906122629)

The browser never chooses a region: on insert the trigger overwrites whatever the client sent, and only trusted server-side functions (public booking, region provisioning) pass a region through a transaction-local setting. On update, region_id is immutable for everyone except super-admins.

The riskiest part was the policies, so I did them in two moves. In phases 6a–6d, I added a new _rgn_ policy for every operation on every table, written in its final form: the existing role condition AND can_access_region(region_id). Because Postgres combines permissive policies with OR (PostgreSQL: Row Security Policies), the old policies still granted the same access, so nothing changed for users. Work orders went last, because every page touches them.

Then a single migration dropped the old policies, keeping a short list of deliberate exceptions: the public booking insert, staff reading their own record, and the personal notifications inbox. A count check in the step before had already flagged a real problem: every inventory transaction had no location, so none of them had a region, and the new policy would have hidden all of them. So the tightening migration first made that policy accept location-less rows, then dropped the old ones. Before applying it, I ran it inside a transaction as the owner and rolled it back. The dry run showed the expected counts, and I applied it for real.

Opening Jhelum on 7–8 September turned up three more issues. The first two came from the row-count check, not from clicking around:

Every region-owned table follows one pattern. This is the work orders UPDATE policy, slightly simplified:

work_orders update policy (slightly simplified)

Authorisation never comes from a value the client sends. The region comes from auth.uid(), the user ID inside the JWT that Supabase has already verified, looked up against user_regions on the server.

Child tables follow their parent through a second SECURITY DEFINER helper, region_of_work_order(id), so an attachment is visible only if its work order's region is.

What I'd change (recommended, not yet done):

PostgreSQL is explicit: "Referential integrity checks, such as unique or primary key constraints and foreign key references, always bypass row security" (PostgreSQL: Row Security Policies).

In practice, if invoices.work_order_id is a plain foreign key, a user in region A who knows the ID of a region B work order can create an invoice in region A that points at it. RLS checks the invoice's own region_id, which is A, so the insert passes. The foreign key check only asks "does this work order exist?", without RLS.

Status in ZCS: child tables inherit their parent's region through policy joins, but the foreign keys between region-owned tables are still plain. Same-region composite foreign keys were deferred during the migration. UUIDs make guessing impractical, but IDs leak through URLs and exports, so I treat this as an open gap. I haven't tested it on the real app.

The fix is to make the foreign key carry the region:

In the companion demo (plain PostgreSQL 16 with a Supabase-style mock, not a real Supabase stack), swapping this back to a plain foreign key made the isolation test fail on exactly this case.

Done, before the switch: on a local Supabase stack and on the migrated copy, I worked through every table, form and field by hand, including a newly created region. I didn't keep a written checklist of those runs, so I'm not listing individual results.

Done, on the migrated project (now the live one):

Not done yet: a two-region test where an Islamabad user and a Jhelum user each try to read, insert, update, delete, move and cross-link the other's data. That's the next test I'd add:

The companion demo does this on a simplified four-table schema: eight checks, including a super-admin moving a work order and its invoice following along. All eight pass. I also planted one leak per run to show the test can fail. A plain foreign key, an extra using (true) policy, a region helper that always says yes, and an anon grant were each caught. Making the stamping trigger a no-op did not fail the test, because WITH CHECK blocked the cross-region writes on its own. That's a useful result in itself: the trigger keeps data correctly filed; RLS is the security boundary.

Adding branches to a live Supabase app came down to a small number of database rules, applied in an order where each step could be checked and undone. The SQL was the easy part. The practical takeaway: write down your row counts before you start, and treat any count that changes unexpectedly as a stop sign, because under RLS a mistake doesn't throw an error. It just shows less data.

Is the Supabase publishable (anon) key safe to put in the browser? Yes, it's designed to be public, but only because Row Level Security decides what it can reach. With RLS off or a policy missing, anyone holding the key can read that table through the Data API. The secret (service_role) key bypasses RLS and must stay on the server.

Is region or branch isolation the same as multi-tenancy? No. Multi-tenancy separates different organisations that must never see each other's data. Branch isolation separates parts of one company, often with super-users who see everything and some shared data. Both use the same tools: an ID column, a membership table and RLS policies.

Do foreign keys bypass RLS in Supabase? Yes. PostgreSQL runs foreign key checks without applying row security, so a row can reference another branch's row if its ID is known. Including the branch column in the foreign key, (region_id, parent_id) → parent(region_id, id), keeps references inside one branch.

How do I test Supabase RLS policies? Act as a real user by switching to the authenticated role with that user's JWT claims, never as postgres or service_role. Try to read, insert, update, delete and link another branch's rows, and assert on row counts, because RLS hides rows without raising errors. Also confirm each user still sees their own data.

Can I add a region_id column to a live Supabase table safely? Adding a nullable column with no default only changes PostgreSQL's catalogue and doesn't rewrite the table, though it briefly takes an exclusive lock. Backfill existing rows next, then add NOT NULL, the foreign key and an index, which all validate the data and fail if anything was missed. Rehearse on a copy first and compare row counts before and after.

Code: the foreign key example comes from a simplified companion demo (four tables, not the ZCS schema), run on plain PostgreSQL with a Supabase-style mock.

AI note: I built ZCS with AI coding agents, mainly Claude Code, alongside Cursor and Antigravity. I made the design decisions, ran each migration and checked it with the counts described here.

This post first appeared on my site: Branch isolation in a live Supabase app.

Build a Country Allowlist in Cloudflare Workers and Nginx

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posted on Nov 17 • Originally published at networkdatalabs.com

Product said the app should only be available in the United States and Canada. The first instinct was to drop a country database onto every origin and start returning 403s. Then we remembered the Worker already has a country code on the request, for free, with no extra lookup.

A country gate is a coarse filter: it looks at the IP’s country, not at the person. Behind a known VPN network, a Tor exit or a privacy relay, the country you see is the egress, not the user’s. location.country_code can also be null, so every example below fails open when the country is unknown or the lookup errors.

Use the ISO 3166-1 alpha-2 code (US, CA, …), not the city. City-level data is a best-effort estimate; save it for soft decisions like defaults or language, not a hard 403.

This post starts with request.cf.country, then shows when to look the IP up so Nginx can enforce the same rule. The longer version is the guide to blocking countries by IP in Workers and Nginx.

For the current visitor, Workers already give you a country code on request.cf.country. If all you need is “allow the United States and Canada, otherwise 403”, use it. That is the right default, and it does not spend API quota.

Reach for GET /v0/ip/{ip} when Nginx at the origin has to enforce the same policy (there is no request.cf there), when you want one source of truth for Workers and origin, or when you’re classifying IPs that are not the current visitor (logs, webhooks). Country is part of geolocation, which is on the Free plan: 1,000 requests per UTC calendar month, 1 request/second, for personal and non-commercial use. Cache per IP or you’ll burn the quota on repeat visitors. Commercial use needs a paid plan; paid plans start at $9/month.

The country code, name, continent and is_eu fields are documented on the IP to Country API page.

Store the key with npx wrangler secret put NDL_API_KEY, not in wrangler.toml. The visitor IP is in CF-Connecting-IP. Cache the JSON per IP in the Cache API, keep a tight timeout and fall back to request.cf.country (or allow) if the call fails:

The Cache API is per data center and costs nothing extra. If you want one shared cache across locations, store the JSON in Workers KV with expirationTtl instead. Use request.cf.country when you only need the current visitor’s country at the edge; use the API when origin must apply the same rule, or when you’re looking up IPs that aren’t this request. The two can occasionally disagree, so pick one source for the gate and stick to it.

Nginx doesn’t have the visitor country on its own unless you add a geo module or a local database. A small sidecar keeps the policy in one place: Nginx asks it on each request via auth_request, and the sidecar looks up GET /v0/ip/{ip}, caches the result and returns 200 (allow) or 403 (deny). The sidecar must fail open too: API errors return 200, and if the sidecar itself is down, Nginx maps 502/503/504 to 200.

Pass a single client IP in X-Client-IP. If Nginx is at the edge, that’s $remote_addr. If Cloudflare is the only proxy in front of Nginx, use $http_cf_connecting_ip. Don’t parse raw X-Forwarded-For — the client can put any address there.

auth_request_set copies X-IP-Country from the sidecar onto $ip_country, so the origin still sees the country after an allow. Skip the gate on health checks and static assets if you can, so those paths never spend a lookup (the in-process cache already covers repeat IPs).

Cache per IP for a few hours in the sidecar and in the Worker. Keep timeouts tight (500–800 ms) and always fail open on errors. Country is on the Free plan, but 1,000 requests/month is not enough to look up every request without a cache. For log backfill, POST /v0/bulk (paid plans) accepts up to 100 IPs per request; each IP counts toward the monthly quota.

If the user is on a VPN, you get the egress country. is_vpn (known VPN networks), is_tor and is_relay (privacy relay) are paid-plan flags on the same response if you want to treat that traffic differently. They are not required for a basic country gate.

Google’s Spark AI agent becomes ‘Tasks’ — and now it’s free

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this topic will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Tasks in Gemini allows the AI agent to call businesses on your behalf, purchase items with Google Pay, and more.

Tasks in Gemini allows the AI agent to call businesses on your behalf, purchase items with Google Pay, and more.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Posts from this author will be added to your daily email digest and your homepage feed.

Google is rebranding its Spark AI agent as “Tasks” and making it free to use. The feature, which is powered by Google’s new Gemini 4 Hydrogen AI model, can do things like create meal plans, manage your calendar, and dig through your emails to find recurring expenses you might not need.

Users in the US will get access to a few other features, including the ability for the AI agent to call businesses to compare product pricing and check inventory, purchase items with Google Pay, and sign into accounts that support Google Password Manager. US users can also assign Gemini tasks by emailing gemini@google.com or texting it.

Tasks in Gemini is just one of the many agentic AI features that have emerged over the past year, including Claude Duet, OpenAI’s Dots, and Meta’s Muse. Google says Gemini will perform tasks in a “hardware-isolated virtual machine that’s dedicated to your account and never shared with other users.” You can also provide instructions for Gemini while it completes tasks. When performing more sensitive tasks, like purchasing an item, it will stop working and ask for your permission before following through.

Freenome (FRNM) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Freenome's first public quarter brings a $180 million FDA-driven revenue jump.

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Freenome, Inc. (FRNM -0.50%) transitioned to a public entity during the quarter, focusing its strategic efforts on the commercial rollout of its inaugural FDA-approved colorectal cancer screening test and the expansion of its multiomics detection pipeline. Management indicated that the company's business model relies on a dual-track commercial strategy, combining a large-scale partnership for primary care reach with an internal team focused on health system integration. The company stated that the clinical data generated from its initial commercial tests will serve as a foundational data set to refine its artificial intelligence models and accelerate the development of subsequent single-cancer and multi-cancer screening products. Management reported that the establishment of payer coverage and the integration of tests into electronic medical record workflows are the primary operational priorities for the current launch phase.

Operator: Good afternoon, and thank you for standing by. Welcome to Freenome's third quarter 2026 earnings conference call. All participants will be able to listen only until the question-and-answer portion of this call. During the question-and-answer session, you will be able to ask your question by pressing star one on your touchtone phone. This call is being recorded by Freenome, with the exception of any participant's questions asked during the question-and-answer session. The entire call, including the question-and-answer session, is material copyrighted by Freenome. It cannot be recorded, rebroadcast without Freenome's express written permission. I would now like to introduce Mr. Bill Taggart, Director of Corporate Communications.

Bill Taggart: Good afternoon, and thank you for joining us. With me today are Aaron Elliott, Chief Executive Officer, Riley Ennis, Co-Founder and Chief Product Officer, and Linh Le, Chief Financial Officer. They will provide opening remarks, and following their comments, we will take your questions. Before we begin, please note that this call will include forward-looking statements under the safe harbor provisions of the Private Securities Litigation Reform Act of 1995. Please refer to our most recent SEC filings for important risk factors that could cause our actual performance and results to materially differ from those expressed or implied in these forward-looking statements.

Any forward-looking statements made on this call represent our views only as of today, and we disclaim any obligation to update any forward-looking statements. During this call, we will refer to certain non-GAAP financial measures, including non-GAAP net income, adjusted EBITDA, and free cash flow. These non-GAAP measures should be considered in addition to, and not as a substitute for or superior to, our results prepared in accordance with GAAP. Reconciliations of these non-GAAP measures to the most directly comparable GAAP measures are included in our earnings press release issued today, which is available in the Investors section of our website at investors.freenome.com. Now, let me hand it to Aaron.

Aaron Elliott: All right. Good afternoon, everybody. Thank you for joining our first earnings call as a public company. Today, we are really proud to introduce Freenome and share with you how we are working to bring our AI-enabled cancer screening platform to millions of patients. We really look forward to establishing an ongoing dialogue with the investment community as we execute on this mission. In July, we achieved a major milestone by going public on the Nasdaq under the ticker FRNM, raising approximately $310 million in gross proceeds. This couldn't have been possible without our incredible team, our clinical and commercial partners, the patients in our clinical studies, and the ongoing support of our shareholders.

As we begin this new chapter as a public company, we are focused on building out our commercial infrastructure and advancing our broad product pipeline. We are incredibly excited about the potential of our technology to truly transform the landscape of cancer screening. Now, since some of you are getting to know Freenome for the first time, I want to start with just a brief overview of who we are. We were founded in 2014 on a pretty simple premise, that the traditional approach to cancer screening is highly inefficient. Today's testing options are fragmented and burdensome, requiring patients to navigate completely different processes for different cancers.

As a result, a large percentage of eligible people never get screened at all. We've turned this premise into our mission, to make early detection simple, accessible, and affordable so cancer can be caught at its earliest, most treatable stages. Over the past decade, we've built a proprietary AI-driven multiomics discovery platform that has the ability to look at numerous biomarkers, including DNA and protein signatures, and incorporate the signals into our tests that are most informative for each cancer type. This platform serves as the foundation of our expanding portfolio of blood-based tests, which began with colorectal cancer, now extends to lung cancer, and will soon include other single and multi-cancer tests.

By aligning screenings with an individual's risk profile, we believe we're uniquely positioned to scale AI-driven diagnostics, and we call this approach Personalized Cancer Detection. The data generated from these tests will really be key. Beginning with an FDA-approved reimbursed CRC test enables the collection of massive population-level molecular datasets and the establishment of a longitudinal relationship with the patient. This data, we believe, will rapidly improve existing tests, confirm efficacy for other cancer types, and enable our potential expansion into broader disease areas beyond cancer. Now, it's been a pretty busy quarter for us.

In addition to going public, we achieved numerous other milestones, including the FDA approval and commercial launch of our first test, SimpleScreen CRC, validation of an updated version of that test with improved performance, FDA Breakthrough Device Designation for our lung cancer test, and then most recently, the commercial launch of our SimpleScreen Lung LDT. In July, the FDA approved SimpleScreen CRC based on data from PREEMPT CRC, which was the largest prospective clinical validation study ever conducted for a blood-based colorectal cancer screening test. While colorectal cancer is highly preventable when caught early, nearly half of the 120 million eligible U.S. adults still remain unscreened.

We believe the simplicity and convenience of a blood test can really overcome traditional barriers to testing and dramatically increase screening adherence. The full results of PREEMPT CRC were published in JAMA in June 2025, and in a pre-specified analysis adjusted to the U.S. Census, SimpleScreen CRC detected colorectal cancer with 81.1% sensitivity at 90.4% specificity. Also in this quarter, we released top-line data for an updated version of SimpleScreen CRC. This is our version 2 test, and we were able to do it within one year of our first version, really demonstrating our ability to rapidly iterate on our technology and improve performance in detecting advanced precancerous lesions, which is really the most important.

Riley will speak more to that here in a minute. Now, let's turn to commercial. Our unique commercial model is to efficiently and cost-effectively scale SimpleScreen adoption by leveraging strategic partnerships, like the Abbott partnership for SimpleScreen CRC when it is ordered as a standalone test, and Roche for international kitting, combined with our internal sales team focused on targeting the approximately 600 health systems in the U.S. that are dedicated to increasing patient screening rates. This combination of partner-driven, bottoms-up awareness of SimpleScreen and top-down health system channel strategy is how we plan to scale our commercial foundation.

Freenome maintains full U.S. commercial rights when SimpleScreen CRC is ordered in tandem with any other Freenome test where the patient meets criteria. That means we receive the full CRC reimbursement for those tests. This partnership structure was deliberate, as we anticipate the market will naturally shift towards risk-based multi-cancer screening from a simple blood draw. The data really supports this. 84% of the 120 million people eligible for CRC testing also qualify for another cancer screening test. Of course, you then get almost complete overlap when pairing with an asymptomatic MCED test.

So we believe our Personalized Cancer Detection approach, layered on top of Abbott's reach into primary care with CRC, will really allow us to capture a disproportionate share of the value as multi-cancer screening becomes more common. Abbott holds exclusive U.S. commercialization rights for standalone CRC testing, backed by a licensing arrangement worth up to $885 million in potential payments to Freenome, tied to regulatory market access achievements and funding for joint research and development. To date, we have received $242.2 million of these payments, including most recently the $100 million milestone for CRC version one FDA approval.

Most importantly, to capitalize on the potential long-term success of the test and maintain financial upside, we also receive a 10% royalty on net sales once a 20% gross margin is achieved. Abbott provided many of the attributes that we were looking for in a partner, including one of the largest primary care sales channels, and importantly, the ability to reach the unscreened patient population with high efficiency. By scaling the SimpleScreen brand, Abbott effectively primes the market for our SimpleScreen commercial portfolio while generating the critical test data required to fuel our pipeline of next-generation AI-enabled cancer diagnostic tests.

Abbott began marketing and selling SimpleScreen CRC on September 30th, integrating the test into its existing colorectal cancer screening portfolio and drawing on its established relationships with primary care providers and health systems nationwide. We are only about a month into launch, so it is too early to really provide any specific volume guidance, but Abbott has trained its approximately 800-person sales force and begun the reach out to their 380,000 provider channel. At this stage of launch, the focus is really on EMR integrations, health system and provider engagement, putting in place the proper ordering workflows, and engaging payers on coverage.

As of now, more than 2,000 clinical sites have requested blood collection kits, showing early interest, and we are watching this test conversion closely. We are currently working with Abbott to put together the commercial KPIs that we believe best track progress and future potential. We are highly encouraged by the recent momentum surrounding blood-based CRC testing, which has secured pivotal inclusions in both clinical guidelines and major insurance policies. This progress is highlighted by the American Cancer Society's recent August update, which officially added SimpleScreen CRC as an approved screening option for average-risk adults.

On the payer side, in addition to Medicare coverage, which set an initial price for SimpleScreen at $920, UnitedHealthcare led the industry by becoming the first major commercial insurer to establish coverage for blood-based CRC testing, really marking a crucial milestone towards widespread commercial acceptance. Carelon revised its clinical guidelines to cover SimpleScreen CRC as a medically necessary second-line option every three years for average-risk individuals. Cigna, most recently, with EviCore, one of the nation's largest specialty benefit managers, will implement a policy covering blood-based CRC screening across the 20 states that mandate alignment with ACS guidelines. Together, these developments represent a transformative shift for broader market access and patient adoption.

Separately, in September, HHS announced new appointments to the U.S. Preventive Services Task Force, really an encouraging step as it brings USPSTF back to full strength and enables it to resume its important work. We look forward to the future developments of this body. In addition to our commercial partnership with Abbott in the U.S., we have an exclusive license and option agreement with Roche to develop a kitted version of our multi-cancer test outside of the U.S. We believe this partnership can meaningfully expand global access to blood-based cancer screening over time, while Freenome retains ex-U.S. multi-cancer centralized testing rights. Beyond CRC, we recently reached a significant milestone by launching our SimpleScreen Lung test.

This test is designed for adults age 50-80 years with a minimum 20 pack-year smoking history who are currently not up to date with annual low-dose CT screening. While nearly 15 million Americans qualify for lung cancer screening, fewer than 20% are actually up to date. We believe SimpleScreen Lung addresses this gap by offering physicians a practical and convenient screening option for their patients. Though we're just at the beginning of the launch, we're already engaged in encouraging discussions with health systems across the country. Our top-line results released last month demonstrated 85% sensitivity at 50% specificity, including 82% sensitivity for stage 1 disease and a 99.8% negative predictive value.

Looking ahead, we are actively advancing enrollment for our PROACT LUNG registrational study, which remains on track to support a future FDA submission. With that, I'd like to turn it over to Riley, who's going to walk you through the capabilities of our platform and how it's driving our long-term single cancer and multi-cancer test portfolio.

Riley Ennis: Thank you, Aaron. It took us nearly a decade to develop our first FDA-approved CRC test. Within a year, we upversioned the assay and algorithm, demonstrating improved performance, expanded into lung cancer, and have two additional indications reading out before the end of the year. I share these examples as they reflect what we set out to build when we started Freenome, an AI-driven multiomics platform that interrogates multiple analyte technologies and improves over time, and enables an expanding portfolio of early detection tests, while working closely with health systems to build a longitudinal real-world dataset through embedded digital products that can provide a long-term view of an individual's health.

I want to walk through three of our core pillars that are illustrating how Freenome's approach is beginning to deliver. First, how we approach test versioning. The next-generation CRC test incorporated a comprehensive set of assay and algorithm improvements. We ultimately 3x the number of unique molecules derived through our assay, focusing on improving the detection of advanced precancerous lesions. In our pivotal study, the next-generation SimpleScreen CRC met all pre-specified primary and secondary endpoints in the U.S. census-adjusted analysis. High-grade dysplasia sensitivity increased from 30.5% to 41.9%, approximately 11% increase. The overall advanced precancerous lesion sensitivity increased from 13.7% to 18.2%, approximately a 5% increase.

CRC sensitivity was 80.4%, in line with the first version, and all at a 90% specificity among participants with no finding on a colonoscopy. Finding and removing advanced precancerous lesions creates an opportunity to prevent colorectal cancer. Our peer-reviewed health economic model, COSMO-CRC, projects 143 fewer CRC-related deaths and 1,582 additional life years gained per 100,000 people screened compared with the first version. These modeled outcomes demonstrate a meaningful difference when you think about the 60 million people who are currently not screened for CRC. We plan to submit a supplemental PMA by year-end and anticipate approval in the second half of 2027.

Approval and successful technology transfer to Abbott would trigger a $70 million milestone payment, currently expected in the first half of 2028. The second pillar is how we tune our platform for different indications across our Personalized Cancer Detection pipeline. A blood sample contains millions of DNA fragments, RNA, proteins, and other biological information. The earliest cancer signals are only present in a small fraction of that molecular background, and cancer signals can be scarce and differ across indications, stages, and individuals. We built our platform to address this complexity. We measure individual omics at high resolution, such as our DNA methylation assay that quantifies epigenetic changes at a single base level. We also approach our discovery process with breadth.

For example, the comprehensive profiling of non-NGS proteins and other secreted analytes. These are just a subset of the internal/external technologies that we evaluate to identify potentially additive signal and ultimately the best integrated technology. We then use several machine learning and deep learning architectures to incorporate complementary information across each of the biological inputs and tune those models based on the specific populations and clinical need. Our lung LDT product demonstrates this flexibility. Protein signatures provided the strongest stage 1 performance among the technologies we evaluated. This resulted in the launch of our LDT, while in parallel, we continue to optimize the multiomics version of our lung IVD, which recently received FDA Breakthrough status.

We plan to validate this final multiomics version through our PROACT Lung study, which will complete enrollment in the first half of 2027 and read out toward the end of 2027. Beyond CRC and lung, our current SimpleScreen assay embedded information from 10 additional cancer types, providing a foundation that we are building upon for the future single cancer and multi-cancer tests. Those indications we are targeting account for approximately 65% of cancer diagnoses and 70% of cancer-related death. We expect our next two single cancer readouts in December, and our first asymptomatic multi-cancer detection readout in mid-2027. Our third pillar is our learning engine.

Reimbursed CRC screening, Abbott's reach, and established data sharing and linking capabilities gives us a path to build a multimodal molecular and clinical dataset for millions of screening encounters over time. PREEMPT illustrates this potential. We are linking our molecular measurements collected throughout the CRC screening study and have roughly five years of real-world longitudinal follow-up. A preliminary analysis of the clinical data identified 2,000 incidental findings of other cancers. We can now go back and investigate whether signatures were present in the early stages of disease and in those original blood samples. We can also envision using different cohorts for validation of those other indications.

This really shows what's possible as we think about the commercial data flywheel as the training corpus continues to grow. Those larger datasets also play an important role as we think about scaling into other more complex models. Based on a fragment-level deep learning architecture that our team developed and data presented at ICML and a special conference at AACR, we observed for the first time that the fragment-level deep learning model outperformed our linear baseline machine learning model. This is important because these datasets are still small and will continue to grow over time. The real-world data and clinical information also has a role in how we think about expanding access and identification of patients through the screening journey.

As Aaron mentioned, when you look at the CRC screening population, around 84% of people would be eligible for another cancer screening test. When you look at the overlap of different single cancer, multi-cancer screening tests with the CRC screening population, around 84% of people are eligible for another cancer screening test. For example, when you look at a single indication like lung, about 15%-20% of people were eligible for both CRC and lung. We think about the individual indications as well as an asymptomatic multi-cancer test, this really allows us to offer multiple tests to the same individual and expands our impact as our test menu also grows.

When we work with our health system partners, we really see the convergence of the real-world data linkage and our health system strategy. We have been building a set of digital products through Freenome Digital Health, which really empowers our partners to improve the identification of eligible patients, engage them in the screening journey, and support the necessary screening follow-up and navigation. Embedding these into the clinical workflows also allows us to collect the necessary data and additional data that can augment and improve as we think about incorporating those data for future products or test improvements.

Taken together, our North Star is to generate the most impact from a single patient sample and collect data over time that allows us to derive better versions of our tests, and we are excited by this next chapter to bring our technology to millions of people. I will now turn the call over to Linh to go through the financial results for the quarter.

Linh Le: Thank you, Riley, and good afternoon, everyone. Let me start with our financial results for the third quarter ended September 30, 2026. Total revenue was $183.5 million, compared with $1 million in the third quarter of 2025. License and collaboration revenue was $182.5 million. This was driven primarily by $180 million recognized under our collaboration and license agreement following FDA approval of SimpleScreen CRC. That $180 million included $157.5 million for the exclusive commercialization license, along with $22.5 million cumulative catch-up adjustment to revenue recognized in earlier periods. Service and other revenue was $0.9 million. Cost of service was $0.5 million, compared with $0.6 million in the third quarter of 2025.

Turning to operating expenses, total expense were $113 million for the quarter, compared with $65.7 million in the third quarter of 2025. Research and development expense was $68.4 million, up $17.8 million from the third quarter of 2025. General and administrative expense was $44.6 million, up $29.6 million over the same period. These increases primarily reflect non-cash stock-based compensation. This included $9.9 million in research and development and $29.8 million in general and administrative expense related to restrictive stock awards. The service condition for those awards were satisfied at the closing of the business combination. Operating income was $70 million, compared with an operating loss of $65.3 million in the third quarter of 2025.

Net income was $60.1 million, or $0.58 per share. This compares with a net loss of $64.5 million, or $0.95 per share in the third quarter of 2025. We use 103.2 million weighted average diluted shares outstanding to calculate net income per share. Now turning to our non-GAAP measures. Adjusted EBITDA was $117.9 million for the quarter, compared with a -$56.3 million in the third quarter of 2025. Free cash flow, also a non-GAAP measure, was $35.7 million, compared with -$44.8 million in the third quarter of 2025. This quarter's free cash flow includes the $100 million milestone payment from Abbott. Turning to the balance sheet, we remain well capitalized.

We ended the third quarter with approximately $430 million in cash equivalent, and marketable securities. This includes the net proceeds from our July business combination and concurrent private placement, as well as the $100 million milestone payment received from Abbott. We continue to expect our current cash resources to fund our operations into 2028. As a reminder, our agreement with Abbott provides for up to $885 million in total potential value. Of that amount, we have already received $242.2 million. The remaining $642.8 million is contingent on future regulatory and market access milestones and other conditions. Royalties on test sales are in addition to that amount. Finally, let me turn to our outlook for the remainder of 2026.

We expect fourth quarter revenue of $2 million-$4 million. This would bring full-year revenue to between $192 million-$194 million. With the commercial launch still in its early stages, we do not expect meaningful testing revenue or royalties in the fourth quarter. Our revenue guidance reflects three components. First, research and development fees from Abbott for joint work under our agreement. Second, service revenue from our U.K. operations. And third, a modest contribution from lab service fees associated with Abbott's commercial launch of SimpleScreen CRC. As a reminder, lab service fees represent Abbott's reimbursement of our cost to process SimpleScreen CRC tests in our labs.

This revenue is gross margin neutral, and its contribution will depend largely on the pace of Abbott's launch. I will now turn the call over to the operator for Q&A. Operator?

Operator: Thank you. Ladies and gentlemen, if you have a question or a comment at this time, please press star one on your telephone. If your question has been answered or you wish to remove yourself from the queue, please press star one again. We will pause for a moment while we compile our Q&A roster. Our first question comes from Puneet Souda with Leerink Partners. Your line is open.

Puneet Souda: Yeah. Hi, guys. Congrats here on the first quarter as a public company, and couple of questions here. First one, just wanted to clarify, in that $157 million, could you provide what is there beyond the milestone payments? Apologize if I missed that, and I have a few follow-ups after.

Linh Le: Sure. Thanks for the question, Puneet. That $167.5 relates to the commercial contract we have with Abbott, and as a result of the $100 million for the V1 we received, that trigger recognition of that amount.

Puneet Souda: Okay. Maybe the second question is, and I could follow up on the more details later, but maybe the second question is just in terms of the, look, $2 million-$4 million for the fourth quarter, that is understandable given the milestone payments this year. You have milestone payments coming in next year as well, as well as the products are going to be ramping. Maybe just give us some thoughts on 2027, if you could.

Aaron Elliott: Yeah. Thanks, Puneet. This is Aaron. For next year, if you think about it from a partnership perspective, we will have R&D contribution to revenue. Some of this will depend on where some of the other milestones could potentially fall. Right now, we're guiding for the V2 into 2028. As of now, we're not giving guidance for Abbott as it relates to the service fee revenue and our own internal revenue. For the next earnings call, we will likely provide guidance for 2027 from a commercial standpoint.

Puneet Souda: Got it. Okay. Then maybe just last one. In terms of SimpleScreen launch, I mean, you have 800 reps. That's sizable for any product launch. Maybe just give us how you're thinking about now with the reps trained, how should we think about the momentum there, and just given how the existing Abbott products are and how this fills the bag for the reps. Thank you.

Aaron Elliott: Yeah. I would say, we are only about a month into the launch, so a lot of the activities right now is really, I would say, laying the foundation for long-term growth. It's really three things. One, the engaging and educating of providers. How does blood-based testing fit within their workflows and fit within ACS guidelines? So part of that is obviously training and deploying those 800 sales reps and just that whole sort of Abbott commercial engine around that education piece. Another big part of the activities ongoing is the negotiation with payers, not just from a cover standpoint, but even the payers that have put policies out there, negotiating pricing for those payers.

As you know, that takes some time to do, and Abbott is actively engaged with most of the top 20 payers currently, and we do expect in the next couple quarters to be able to announce some successes there. Then finally, and maybe the biggest one is, the activities around integrating with EMRs and getting health system workflows in place, and that's really to drive bulk ordering. Those activities are ongoing and picking up momentum. I do think one encouraging sign as of now that we are releasing is the 2,000 clinical sites that have ordered SimpleScreen collection kits in the first 30 days. We think that's a good sort of leading indicator as those start converting over to volume.

We do expect significant traction heading into 2027. We are working with Abbott pretty closely on what commercial KPIs beyond volume that we would communicate to you all to reflect progress, and we will have a better update for that on the next earnings call.

I know it is super early, but have your expectations changed on timing given the current trajectory you have observed post-launch? The current $920 price, how are you viewing the opportunity to pursue an ADLT rate, and could a higher Medicare price move the royalty timing meaningfully?

Aaron Elliott: Yeah. Thanks for the question, Subbu. You are correct that we have a 10% royalty that triggers on a 20% gross margin. Before that, we receive 50% of the gross margin, and as of now, there are no expectations on timing as it relates to royalty. Obviously, royalty is a function of cost of goods with scales with volume, but also ASP. Abbott is in charge of driving ASP with their commercial payers and also dictating the coding strategy for Medicare for SimpleScreen CRC. The $920 rate, I think with Abbott, one thing we are trying to balance is what is an appropriate price that in the long term can really drive blood-based test adoption.

That was part of the thinking going into coding strategy and pricing strategy as it relates to Medicare. So that is why we went that direction initially with Abbott. We will readdress coding with the V2 test as it relates to potentially ADLT.

Subbu Nambi: Thank you for that. That is helpful. My follow-up, what drove the decision to launch the protein-only assay now ahead of the multiomics version for lung? How do you plan to move ordering physicians to the multiomics test once it is available, and should we expect the protein-only assay to be retired at that point?

Aaron Elliott: Yeah, I can take the first part, and then Riley can add some additional color. When looking at the performance of the protein-only test, we do think there is some additional upside for our multiomics assay as it relates to performance, and we will show that hopefully on our PROACT readout. But it does provide us a lot of pricing flexibility.

The protein-only test is a fraction of the cost of goods of the multiomics assay, and really in this time period before FDA approval and establish a reimbursement, our focus really is on priming the market with the lung cancer test and getting these patients onto our platform, not just for lung, but also for other indications like CRC and other indications that we have in the pipeline. That drove a big part of the decision when we looked at what assay to roll out initially before there is established reimbursement. I will Riley give additional thoughts.

Riley Ennis: We were very encouraged by the stage 1 sensitivity at 82%, which really highlights the unique nature of some of these secreted analytes for detecting earlier stage disease, and that's what led to the launch of the lung LDT.

Subbu Nambi: Perfect. Super helpful. Thank you.

Aaron Elliott: Thanks, Dan. Right now, when we look at the commercial infrastructure, it's really scheduled to start building out next year. From a commercial standpoint, right now it's about 20 individuals on the commercial side really targeting health system and setting up our platform. I mean, that's the majority of the focus currently. As you would expect, we're not giving guidance on volume for 2027 yet, just the fact that we're only a couple weeks here in the launch. A big focus of ours right now, again, is priming that market with our protein tests and having that sort of pricing flexibility to drive volume.

Dan Brennan: Got it. Okay, thank you. Anyway, just related to that, when we think about attach rates, Guardant has over 50% attach rate so far with the MCD. When we think about attach rates of lung to CRC, any way to at least think about that? What's a reasonable proxy?

Aaron Elliott: Yeah, right now, as Riley mentioned, if you look at the overlap, it is right around 20% overlap between CRC and lung. We are interacting with health systems that are interested in our platform in general, not specifically a single indication. We do anticipate being able to drive that attach rate, but we do not yet have the data to give any sort of trends as far as putting together what that attach rate would look like. We are expecting somewhere in that 20% range based on the data.

Dan Brennan: Got it. Then I know you mentioned the next two single cancer readouts will be in December. I guess you will announce those when they hit, because I know you have talked about in the past esophageal, pancreatic, and liver.
