‘Both utopians and the doomers are going to be disappointed’: Fed advisor Marc Andreessen on AI going from boom to bust

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Marc Andreessen got the last word at the Federal Reserve’s big AI conference in San Francisco on Monday, and he used it to tell the central bank that nearly everyone in the room was going to be let down.

“My line has been both utopians and the doomers are going to be disappointed,” the venture capitalist said at the San Francisco Fed, “specifically disappointed by the scope and magnitude of the real-world impact, and specifically the economic impact.”

Andreessen co-leads the Productivity and Jobs Task Force, which Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh set up to tell the central bank what super intelligence—“SI,” in the White House’s preferred term—will do to growth, prices and jobs. On Monday, Andreessen previewed what he’ll contribute to the group’s report. Without using the name, he described one of the oldest problems in economics: William Baumol’s “cost disease,” the reason flat-screen TVs get cheaper every year while hospital bills, tuition and rent go the other way. His case is that AI won’t cure it, and could even make it worse.

The Fed hasn’t quite laid out that outcome.

The task force is advisory, and Andreessen has no vote on interest rates. He co-leads it with Stanford economist Charles Jones and Microsoft executive Asha Sharma, one of five outside panels the Fed named in June to rethink how the central bank works. Its charge, per the Fed, is to assess the economic impact of “new general-purpose technologies, including super intelligence, to inform the Federal Reserve’s policy judgments.” The panels report to the rate-setting committee with recommendations due by year-end, though Warsh said in October that “some, but not all” would turn their homework in on time. The Fed’s task force page lists no ethics or recusal terms for the panels. Andreessen’s firm, Andreessen Horowitz, raised more than $15 billion in new funds in January, as venture firms raced to keep up with the AI boom.

Andreessen is also Silicon Valley’s best-known accelerationist. His 2023 “Techno-Optimist Manifesto” declared, “We believe in accelerationism,” which he defined as “the conscious and deliberate propulsion of technological development.” It listed @BasedBeffJezos, the pseudonymous account behind the “effective accelerationism,” or e/acc, movement, first among its “patron saints,” and it named the enemy: “Our enemy is stagnation.” When OpenAI’s board fired Sam Altman a month later, Andreessen posted simply “E/acc!” This is the man who told the Fed that stagnation might win out over an AI-powered industrial revolution on Monday.

Warsh opened the day by laying out three “illustrative scenarios” Fed board staff have begun to evaluate. In one, AI lifts productivity, the gains are broadly shared and the economy stays close to full employment. In another, growth still runs above its long-term average, but automation pushes unemployment higher. In the third, AI “could disappoint after significant capital investment and stock market gains,” stocks fall, the economy slows and it eventually recovers “without the productivity gains.”

All three turn on whether AI arrives in force. Andreessen’s version is duller: most of the economy just doesn’t let it in.

He splits the economy in two. The “fast sectors,” like consumer electronics, media and commodity goods, take up new technology quickly, and “what those sectors all have in common is they have rapidly falling prices.” The “slow sectors”—health care, education, housing, and law and government—“are not legally allowed to have rapid change, like literally not.”

The machine “cannot get an MD, cannot prescribe medication,” he said. “The machine cannot get admitted to the bar.” The slow sectors, in his telling, pair restricted supply with subsidized demand: you can’t easily build new housing or open new hospitals, and when voters revolt over the cost, Washington steps in with student loans and housing subsidies. “So, of course, prices can only go up.”

Then he followed it to its end. As prices crash in the fast sectors, they shrink as a share of GDP, and the slow sectors take over. Health care, he said, “is already a fifth of US GDP and I don’t see why it doesn’t go to a fourth, a third and then half and then ultimately 80%.” (The government’s count is 18% in 2024, or $5.3 trillion, per CMS actuaries.)

“You might interpret this as good news because it means the AI revolution is not going to, you know, destroy all the jobs or kill everybody,” he said. “You could also view this as very negative, which is productivity growth just simply may not grow very much. Economic growth just simply may not grow very much. We may just continue to stagnate.”

Andreessen called himself “extremely optimistic,” saying that AI gives the country “a magic formula” and comparing it to being “handed this elixir to boost productivity growth.” But policy is a problem, he suggested. The decisions about what to do with AI, he said, “are going to matter actually way more than what the technology is capable of,” because without them “the technology will simply not get rolled out.”

That landed in a room where, three hours earlier, Warsh had brought up “secular stagnation,” the 1930s worry that economists returned to after the financial crisis, and asked whether superintelligence “dramatically” changes that picture.

Baumol, the late New York University and Princeton economist, worked this out in the 1960s, starting with the economics of the performing arts. A string quartet needs the same four musicians and the same playing time it did in Mozart’s day, but those musicians have to be paid wages set by an economy where factory workers keep getting more productive. So live music, and later education and medicine, keep getting more expensive relative to everything else, and keep taking a bigger share of spending.

In his 2012 book, The Cost Disease, Baumol projected that if health costs kept growing at their recent pace, they would rise from 15% of the average person’s income in 2005 to 62% by 2105. He was an optimist about it. “No matter how painful rising medical and educational bills may be, society can afford them,” he wrote, because productivity elsewhere makes everyone richer. The real threat, he added, was “foolish public policy.”

For Baumol, the disease was built into the work: you can’t speed up a string quartet. For Andreessen, it’s built into the law. That matters for the Fed, because an agency that sets interest rates can’t fix zoning boards or medical licensing.

The economist who has done the most to bring Baumol into the AI debate was in the audience. Jones, who is on leave at Anthropic, stayed quiet because the company is preparing to go public, as Andreessen remarked to the crowd. In a 2017 paper for the National Bureau of Economic Research, Jones, Philippe Aghion and Benjamin Jones argued that “growth may be constrained not by what we are good at but rather by what is essential and yet hard to improve,” crediting the idea to Baumol.

San Francisco Fed President Mary Daly asked a panel of executives where their “weak link” was, and Erik Wexler, CEO of the nonprofit hospital system Providence, gave the slow-sector answer. “We’re part of the weak link,” he said. Providence operates in seven states, and as each writes its own AI rules, “it’s almost like sometimes being in seven different countries.”

Earlier on the same panel, though, Wexler complicated Andreessen’s picture. AI tools that listen in on appointments and write up the notes are saving some physicians “two to four hours of time a day,” he said, and doctors have called it “life-changing.” In 35 years in the industry, he said, he’d never heard them say that about anything else. So the hours are being saved inside a slow sector. The open question is whether they ever show up in the price of a hospital visit.

Andreessen’s forecast cuts against the case Warsh made before he got the job. In a Wall Street Journal op-ed late last year, Warsh wrote that AI “will be a significant disinflationary force, increasing productivity and bolstering American competitiveness,” as Axios reported. In speeches and writing, he argued AI-driven productivity could justify lower interest rates, an echo of Alan Greenspan’s Fed holding off on hikes in the late 1990s.

As chair, he has hedged. “My judgment is we have seen an improvement in productivity; we can’t measure it precisely,” he told reporters on Oct. 28, adding that “most of that change in productivity precedes everything we’re talking about—super intelligence.” The Fed held rates at 3.75% to 4% that day, and a reporter told him most of his colleagues “seem to be on board with raising rates in December.” Warsh declined to prejudge the meeting.

On Monday, Warsh didn’t push back on Andreessen. He restated the argument in Fed terms, as product prices versus services prices, and moved on. He closed the day “cautiously optimistic,” calling it “a hinge point for the US economy,” and said, “I’d rather have our problems than anyone else’s in the world.”

Andreessen has been making this case for years. In 2017 he warned that the slow sectors were “basically just going to eat the economy,” and in March 2023 he wrote that “AI is simply already illegal across most of the economy.” Back then the argument was a rebuttal to the job-loss doomers. On Monday, in front of the Fed chair, it came with the other half of the bargain: if the jobs are safe, the productivity boom may be stuck too.

For companies, the slow sectors are a line item. Workers now pay an average of $7,471 a year toward employer family health coverage, up 9% from 2025, according to KFF’s annual survey. That’s the part of the economy that, by Andreessen’s account, AI isn’t legally allowed to touch.

For the Fed, the question is what kind of disinflation AI brings, if any. Earlier in the day, Warsh asked a panel of economists whether AI might do to services what the China shock did to goods—“productizing services”—and push prices down. Stanford’s Nick Bloom said falling token costs made him “kind of bullish.” Stanford President Jonathan Levin gave Baumol’s answer without the name: health care and education are “the parts of the economy where the disinflationary pressures from automation have not hit,” where “the costs have continued to rise with wages.” Whether that changes depends on what people want, he said, “and there’s also regulation.”

If Andreessen is right, AI’s price cuts land mostly where prices were already falling, and services, where inflation has been hardest to bring down, stay out of reach.

Stripe CEO Patrick Collison sketched the range for the room. At one end sits the old complaint that new technology shows up “everywhere but in the productivity statistics,” a line that goes back to economist Robert Solow in 1987. At the other is a bet between two people Collison described as having “considerable influence” over AI, on whether global energy use doubles in a single month sometime in the next decade. Andreessen is betting on a third outcome: a decade where nothing that dramatic happens, and the prices Americans complain about most keep climbing.

Warsh has asked his task forces for recommendations by year-end, and the Fed’s next rate decision comes Dec. 9. Andreessen put the choice to the central bank as “a political economy” question: “what kind of pace of change do we want, how much productivity growth do we actually want?”

"My first layoff in 14 years making games"

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Yet another round of layoffs has apparently gone down at Tomb Raider studio Crystal Dynamics, as developers report that they've been cut from the studio. This is at least the sixth round of layoffs since the studio last shipped an original game.

Multiple developers have posted on LinkedIn about the layoffs, as reported by Vertical Slice. Those include UI software engineer Garin R. K. Richards and senior game designer Joshua Mitchell. "After several rounds of layoffs at Crystal Dynamics, today it was my turn," Mitchell writes. "My first layoff in 14 years making games."

Also cut is senior environment technical artist Kevin Baker, who led the development of "procedural tools used extensively in the creation of Tomb Raider: Catalyst's game world," and "integrated Claude Code into procedural tool development." We already knew that Legacy of Atlantis is being made with AI, and it seems the studio is keen to use similar generative tech in Catalyst – though not keen enough to keep the people responsible for it employed, I guess.

Crystal Dynamics has not shipped a new game since the release of Marvel's Avengers in 2020. Since then, it was acquired by Embracer Group, a parent company that's undergone no shortage of financial upheaval in recent years. In 2023, Embracer insisted that Crystal Dynamics would be unaffected by the ongoing layoffs at the broader company, a few months before laying off people at the Tomb Raider studio.

Counting today's cuts, there have been five more rounds of layoffs since then. There were three sets of cuts in 2025 alone, both before and after Perfect Dark – which Crystal Dynamics had been co-developing – was cancelled at Xbox. The studio insisted it was still "fully committed" to its upcoming Tomb Raider games when it announced yet more layoffs in March 2026.

The extent of today's cuts are not yet fully clear, nor is their effect on the new games. All three developers who've posted about the layoffs so far listed Catalyst among their credits.

Jensen Huang says he would be 'honoured' to return as Nvidia CEO if board asked him

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Jensen Huang said he would be "honoured" to return as the boss of tech giant Nvidia if the board asked him to, in his first public comments since his shock resignation.

"I created problems for the Board that I deeply regret. I own my mistakes completely," Huang said on X, days after reports he had extramarital affairs, including with an alleged Chinese spy.

He said he had an "outpouring" of calls from company employees, investors and others calling for him to return as CEO of Nvidia.

But, as the BBC reported on Monday, Nvidia's board is not considering bringing him back to lead the company.

The prospect of Huang returning at this point looks unlikely.

The company's board is investigating his conduct, which includes an alleged affair with an Nvidia executive.

The AI chip giant is set to report its financial results later on Tuesday, during which the company's interim chief executive, Collette Kress, may address the situation with Huang.

Beyond the internal Nvidia investigation, Huang's affair with a woman suspected of being an asset for Beijing is set to see the billionaire questioned before multiple committees of US lawmakers in the weeks ahead.

The Nvidia founder said he had "made serious mistakes in my personal life".

Nonetheless, Huang's statement appeared to show some hope he would be able to move past the major corporate scandal.

Huang said people have continued to "reach out to me directly as the company navigates complex technology, market, competitive, and geopolitical dynamics".

"If the board of directors decides that my return is in the best interest of our shareholders, our employees, the company, and America, I would be honoured to answer these calls and return as CEO," Huang added.

Many technology executives in recent days have praised Huang's role in the artificial intelligence boom. A petition began circulating online over the weekend calling for his reinstatement at the company.

Yet, by Monday, many executives were not openly calling for Huang's return.

Brad Gerstner, a major tech investor who knows Huang personally and also holds a significant stake in Nvidia, last week wrote an open letter saying that Nvidia could consider Huang's reinstatement.

On Monday, Gerstner said during an event hosted by Semafor that his letter had been misunderstood and the investigation into Huang's conduct needed to run its course.

While Gerstner questioned the facts around Huang's departure, he added: "I don't know to what degree he misled the board. If I was on a board I would take that very seriously. Let's be clear no one is above the law."

Satya Nadella kicks off Microsoft Ignite with an Outlook outage mea culpa: ‘We have work to do’

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

by Todd Bishop on Nov 17, 2026 at 10:23 amNovember 17, 2026 at 10:23 am

Microsoft CEO Satya Nadella opened his Ignite keynote Tuesday in San Francisco by addressing the elephant in the room: the global Outlook outage that cut off email for people and organizations in more than 70 countries last week.

Before getting to Copilot, agents or any of the event’s news, Nadella said the outage was a reminder of how critical digital infrastructure has become as “the dial tone for the world.”

“We know the trust you place in our services and take that responsibility extremely seriously,” he said. “And in that context, I know we have work to do to provide that resilience that you can count on, the world can count on, and we are committed to ensuring that we learn and we continuously improve.”

The line drew applause from the crowd, interrupting Nadella as he moved to thank the IT pros in the audience who kept their own organizations running through the outage.

It was a brief acknowledgment, and he didn’t go into what went wrong. Microsoft’s preliminary report blamed a bug in a fix it began rolling out in July, which its own automatic rerouting spread until most of the servers in a key Microsoft 365 gateway stopped responding. A final report is due Nov. 20.

From there, Nadella moved on to the main show, calling Copilot “the new operating system for work.” The keynote is ongoing. Watch it above, and stay tuned for updates.

BMW’s first US-built EV promises 456 miles of range and ultra-fast charging

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

The iX5 is BMW’s first fully electric vehicle built in the US, promising an estimated up to 456 miles of range, ultra-fast charging speeds, and plenty of Neue Klasse tech.

BMW officially opened its new Plant Woodruff in South Carolina on November 13, marking the start of series production of its sixth-generation (Gen6) electric vehicle batteries.

The Gen6 batteries will power BMW’s upcoming “X” electric vehicles, starting with the all-new iX5 SUV.

The iX5 is being built about 15 miles away at BMW Group Plant Spartanburg, the company’s first fully electric model to be assembled in the US.

BMW Plant Spartanburg is churning out around 70 iX5s a day following a $1.7 billion investment in South Carolina to expand the plant for EV production and build the new battery assembly plant in Woodruff.

According to Robert Engelhorn, president and CEO of BMW Manufacturing in Spartanburg, the company is confident the iX5 will attract buyers, promising 435 miles of range, ultra-fast charging, and its updated Neue Klasse technology and design.

Engelhorn told Automotive News in an interview that “there’s a clear trend globally for EVs,” adding that “We will contribute to this growth from the BMW side.”

BMW didn’t provide a specific production estimate for the iX5, but suppliers were reportedly told it would be around 60,000 EVs in 2027, down from 100,000 it initially forecasted.

“We said we would begin assembling fully electric BMW vehicles in the US by the end of 2026 – and we are delivering on that commitment,” Engelhorn said.

A company spokesperson confirmed that the iX5 will initially use battery cells from China before switching to US-sourced cells from LG Energy Solutions plant in Arizona by the end of the decade.

With production now underway, the iX5 is expected to begin arriving at US dealerships in early 2027.

The 2027 BMW iX5 60 xDrive starts at $79,800. BMW estimates the 144 kWh battery will provide up to 456 miles of range, up from the 435 miles it previously estimated.

It features BMW’s new “double-X” light signature, which combines the headlights, daytime running lights, side lights, and turn signals into one unit. The Double-X headlights come standard, but BMW said you can switch them for simpler diagonal stripes.

Last month, BMW showcased the i5’s ultra-fast-charging capabilities during a media event in Southern California with IONNA and Alpitronic.

During the demo, the iX5 60 xDrive hit a peak of 464 kW, which BMW said enables it to add an impressive up to 170 miles of range in 10 minutes.

The electric SUV features a dual-motor all-wheel-drive powertrain setup that delivers a combined 578 hp (425 kW) and 593 lb-ft of torque. BMW says it can go from 0 to 60 mph in 4.4 seconds and tow up to 6,000 lbs.

Inside, the iX5 adopts BMW’s Neue Klasse design and tech, like the smaller iX3 SUV. The setup includes a new 17.9″ infotainment touchscreen and BMW’s Panoramic Vision, which spans the bottom of the windshield. There’s also an optional 14.6″ passenger screen for a third display.

Measuring 197.2″ long, 78.7″ wide, and 68.9″ tall, BMW’s new iX5 is about the size of the Porsche Cayenne Electric.

By 2030, BMW plans to be assembling at least fully electric models at Plant Spartanburg. “This marks the beginning of the next chapter in the success story of the largest plant in our global production network,” Raymond Wittman, BMW’s head of production, said.

Following Debrecen (Hungary), Irlbach-Straßkirchen (Germany), and Shenyang (China), Woodruff is the fourth site in BMW’s production network to begin series production of the Gen6 batteries.

How To Watch The Social Reckoning At Home

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

"The Social Reckoning" is finally hitting the home market. It will become available to buy or rent on digital starting November 24, 2026, prior to its physical media release on January 12, 2027.

In case you're unfamiliar with "The Social Reckoning" writ large, here's the gist. Almost two decades after he won an Academy Award for writing the screenplay for "The Social Network," a 2010 dramatization of the founding of Facebook directed by David Fincher, Aaron Sorkin decided to make a sequel that focuses on what Facebook has become in the 2020s. Jesse Eisenberg, who earned an Oscar nod for playing Facebook founder Mark Zuckerberg in "The Social Network," declined to return to the role and was replaced by "Succession" veteran, Emmy and Tony winner, and Sorkin's "The Trial of the Chicago 7" collaborator Jeremy Strong. With Mikey Madison, Jeremy Allen White, Bill Burr, Wunmi Mosaku, Betty Gilpin, and Billy Magnussen starring alongside Strong, the project seemed almost too big to fail before it premiered in theaters on October 9, 2026.

Fail it did, though. Though it reportedly only lost a couple million for Sony (per Deadline), the film absolutely underperformed at the box office, and its rating on Rotten Tomatoes is a middling-to-bleak 54%. So what happened here? Well, from my perspective, "The Social Reckoning" is just a bad movie ... and /Film's chief film critic Chris Evangelista agrees with me. What went wrong as Sorkin tried to make a legacy sequel to one of the best films of the 21st century? Quite a lot actually!

I'll circle back to the "Social Network" of it all shortly, but the bottom line here is that "The Social Reckoning" just ... stinks to high heaven. From the embarrassing prologue featuring Portia Doubleday as a caricature named Kayleigh (I'm not kidding) to the fact that Aaron Sorkin keeps forcing Oscar winner Mikey Madison into outfits that would make her title character from "Anora" blush (in the name of corporate espionage), I found "The Social Network" to be a uniformly dreadful watch from start to finish. Not only is the script clunky as all hell — unfortunately, an ongoing problem with many of Sorkin's recent screenplays — but the story is attempting to make some sort of larger point that doesn't just fall flat but thuds directly to the floor. We already know social media makes everyone angry and insane, and we also know that social media has contributed to right-wing radicalization. Sorkin making a movie that beats us over the head with these two ideas and also ends in a full recreation of the January 6, 2021 insurrection is just as bad as it sounds in theory.

It's also incredible to me that Sorkin managed to cast a lot of great actors and coach them into giving some of the worst performances of their careers. Madison is an enigmatic and compelling performer, and Sorkin transforms her into the robotic Facebook whistleblower Frances Haugen, a deeply unlikable person who doesn't seem to understand human behavior. Jeremy Allen White's journalist Jeff Horwitz is nothing but an exposition machine, and Jeremy Strong's Mark Zuckerberg has no interiority and sounds like Kermit the Frog. The only person who's good in this movie is Bill Burr. Think about that for a second.

"The Social Network" might be one of my very favorite movies of all time, but I can tell you, from a completely unbiased perspective, that "The Social Reckoning" is a wholly undeserving sequel to that 2010 masterpiece. It's clear now, with plenty of hindsight and movies like this, that "The Social Network" was good because of director David Fincher and despite Sorkin. To be fair to Sorkin, he basically admitted that to The Hollywood Reporter after "The Social Reckoning" got soundly panned.

"I would have rather David Fincher directed it — I would rather David Fincher direct everything — but David has an exclusive deal at Netflix, and this was owned by Sony," Sorkin told the outlet on their "Awards Chatter" podcast. Not only did Sorkin also admit that he's "very worried that the parts of directing that [he's] not good at are those parts that you can't learn," he also said, "I was surprised by how little of the science of filmmaking I'd managed to absorb [from other directors] ... I have no visual sensibility, it turns out."

I have to agree with him. Despite the same cinematographer — Jeff Cronenweth — working on both "Network" and "Reckoning," the sequel is visually bland, unimaginatively shot, and even recycles a couple reaction and establishing shots throughout its run time. Basically, "The Social Reckoning" is a poorly made film that I cannot in good conscience recommend; in fact, I'd recommend you just watch "The Social Network" instead. But if you're still interested, you can check out "The Social Reckoning" at home once it's available.

For adventurers and outdoor enthusiasts who want more than the average smartwatch, Garmin sets the standard with its industry-leading features and durability. But while the brand is on many wishlists, its high price tags stop many shoppers from making the splurge. Fortunately, with Amazon’s early Black Friday sale, you can get one of its bestsellers, the 44mm Garmin Instinct 3 AMOLED, for 44% off at $249.99 (originally $449.99).

The 1.2-inch color AMOLED display makes the screen more readable in bright daylight conditions, and battery life is best-in-class, lasting up to 18 days (even longer when you’re not using it regularly), which reduces the need for weekly charging, a must for those going off-grid. And you can’t forget its wildly impressive battery life. At $200 off, it’s a powerful entry into the Garmin world that can accomplish a lot and won’t set you back nearly as much as the brand’s more loaded watches.

The 31 Best Gifts for the Snobbiest People You Know

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

We found the stuff they don’t already own.

This is not meant to sound like a brag, but it inevitably will. Tons of luxurious products come across our desks; as such, I’ve developed especially discerning taste. I’ve tested more than my fair share of $500 colognes, $700 headphones, and $1,000 espresso machines over the years. Not that luxury is synonymous with price, but you know. While these occupational hazards might make me a challenge to shop for, they also give me a discerning eye for gifts that will actually impress my fellow snobs.

If they’re a coffee connoisseur who only drinks single-origin, always espresso consider upgrading their at-home setup with Meraki’s dual boiler espresso machine. For the audiophile whose vinyl collection rivals that of an actual record store, a Fluance turntable will do their records justice. And if their vanity would put a Saks to shame, an under-the-radar Creed fragrance and an award-winning red light mask are worthy additions.

The last thing they want is a bad interface or those ugly Alexa models. Thankfully, 2026 is the year Apple entered the home display market.

My brother has the most extensive at-home espresso setup I’ve ever seen, and he swears by this tamper. It’s spring-loaded to deliver consistent pressure, with an integrated lever for perfectly even pucks. If your espresso pulls aren’t flawless after this, time to look inward.

For a gift that will impress even the most particular of coffee connoisseurs, go with Meraki’s second-gen espresso machine. Featuring a built-in grinder, scales, and a dual boiler system, it’s one of the most premium machines money can buy.

The ultimate snob’s wine opener. A needle and a bit of fancy pump work allow you to pour a glass without ever uncorking the bottle. Great for by-the-glass or sparing yourself any cork emergencies with older vintages.

Take it from someone who actually cooks—the home chef in your life doesn’t want gimmicky gadgets. Instead, gift them practical, high-quality cookware they’ll actually use, like this editor favorite stainless steel frying pan from All-Clad’s D3 line.

For the foodie who worships at the altar of Bourdain, consider gifting them his exact chef’s knife of choice. The Japanese-made Global G-2 has sleek all-metal construction, with a textured handle for a nonslip grip. What’s more, it can be custom-engraved with their name or initials.

For the giftee who’s always complaining that their matcha isn’t the right color (guilty), consider this tea kit. It has everything they need for the perfect cup at home, including a sifter, measuring spoon, whisk, and ceremonial-grade matcha.

Dubbed our top pick for audiophiles, this turntable has it all: consistently great audio quality, a built-in speed control mechanism, and an attractive wood finish.

Aesthetics and audio quality don’t always go hand-in-hand for headphones, which is precisely why this Bang & Olufsen pair is a standout. Our editors named it the best pair of noise-canceling headphones for sound quality, and it’s easily the most attractive model on the list.

If they’ve ever slept at one of the world’s best hotels, they have slept on Frette sheets, worn a Frette robe. Give them that luxury at home.

Every chess player I’ve ever met takes the game extremely seriously, and this is a set they’d be proud to display on their coffee table. Bonus: You can monogram it for a personal touch.

The best home gifts not only spark conversation, but also serve a purpose in their space. Pair this Greek mythology-inspired incense holder with some great-smelling incense (I’m partial to Le Labo), and you have an infallible gift for the artsy types on your list.

All the interior design obsessives I know in Brooklyn (myself included) are obsessed with this lighting brand, which draws from Bauhaus and mid-century modern influences. This retro, saucer-shaped table lamp is a foolproof pick for the giftee who wouldn’t be caught dead turning on “the big light.”

I have to hand it to the cinephiles here: Dolby Atmos is truly superior for watching movies (sorry, films) at home. This Sonos soundbar offers unmatched 3D surround-sound in a sleek package.

A candle is only “impersonal” if it’s ugly or smells bad. Loewe’s incense candle is beautiful, and it smells divine. No snob will ever turn one down.

Sure, Aventus might be Creed’s most popular cologne, but the fragrance snob you know doesn’t want to smell like every other guy. Instead, opt for one of the brand’s under-the-radar scents, like Bois du Portugal. Its notes of lavender, sandalwood, and cedar offer an aromatic scent reminiscent of a vintage barbershop.

Ah, the status hand soap. A gift that works for virtually anyone, especially those with discerning taste. This pick from Nécessaire somehow feels less sceney than other options on the market, and it’s infused with skin-softening ingredients like vitamin B3 and linoleic acid.

As a fragrance expert who tests new scents for a living, few earn me as many compliments as Althair from Parfums de Marly. It’s a grown-up vanilla, with notes of orange blossom, cinnamon, and a dose of smoky guaiac wood to keep it from going saccharine. Although it’s technically from the men’s line, its not-too-sweet gourmand scent profile is truly genderless.

The iRestore red light face mask just won a 2026 Gadget Award. Enough said.

Their parched, damaged strands deserve nothing but the best; in this case, it’s Kérastase’s Oléo-Relax hair mask. It’s got a ton of hydrating ingredients—rose oil, coconut oil, and shorea oil—to replenish dry hair. Not to mention, it smells damn good.

How open source is driving positive change.

Organizations worldwide are incorporating open source methodologies into the way they build and ship their own software.

Helpful tips on securing web applications.

Learn about core challenges in DevSecOps, and how you can start addressing them with AI and automation.

Discover how we built the open Agent Host Protocol to keep your agent sessions accessible across editors, apps, phones, and the cloud.

Models are now good enough to work for hours or even days on their own. You don’t have to watch over every thing they do, but you still need to be able to check in, no matter where you are. An agent can’t live only inside one application. The work should continue even If you close a window or switch devices.

In GitHub Copilot you can start an agent session in one place and pick it up in another. This session continuity runs on the Agent Host Protocol (AHP), an open standard built first in VS Code and adopted across GitHub. Here’s how it works, what we learned building it, and how you can use it to bring your agents into your own apps and workflows.

GitHub Copilot was first built in VS Code as an extension that bundled its own harness. This gave you access to your agents in VS Code, but it tied their lifetime to the editor. Extensions are attached to a single VS Code window. Close that window, and the agent is gone. Update VS Code, and every agent stops as the application restarts. Connect to a remote machine, and you get an entirely new extension host commit-locked against the version of the VS Code workbench you have.

At the same time, the Copilot app was being developed, alongside the existing GitHub mobile apps for iOS and Android. Each product was doing its own distinct integration with cloud-based agents to work towards delivering session continuity.

Each of these integrations solved the problem for one product, but developers also still want to be able to pull their agents into their own workflows–whether that’s an editor, a terminal, a phone, or a tool they built themselves–and they can’t do that if every client needs its own one-off integration. That’s why developers needed a protocol: a standard, independently versioned language across versions of VS Code, the GitHub Copilot app, mobile apps, and any other client that might host an agent. A shared protocol means every client sees the same agents, history, and state without bespoke integrations, and you aren’t tied to one app’s idea of what an agent session is.

The protocol needed to allow multiple clients to interact with the same set of agents seamlessly. Start a session in the cloud from your phone, for example, then continue it from your laptop. It should tolerate network interruptions, reconnections, and concurrent client mutations. And it needed to be full-featured enough for you to do real work from each of your clients.

At the same time, the protocol needed to stay out of agent behavior. Unlike the domains that produced the Language Server Protocol and Debug Adapter Protocol, the world of agents is evolving quickly. That’s why AHP avoids encoding how agents work, and instead focuses on how you want to interact with them.

You also need the protocol to be simple enough to implement on your own client or server. While the full feature set would necessarily be extensive, the core protocol needed to be minimal.

The result of these requirements is the Agent Host Protocol (AHP). It is oriented around URI-identified “channels”, which are the coarsest unit of functionality in the protocol. Every server must implement the root, session, and chat channels, but can also implement more channels that provide terminal support, MCP additions, or OTel reporting, for example.

Within each channel is a reducer-based state object, which drives most of the logic for clients. The server is the arbiter of the state, and each client sends a sequence of actions that either mutate the state or trigger side effects, like sending a chat message. This provides a few very nice properties. Reconnection and replay in poor network conditions are easy: a client sends all queued actions, presents the right ‘loading’ state, and waits for the server to reconcile and acknowledge the actions before confirming them. Clients and servers are straightforward to debug. And reducer-based state also plays nicely with popular UI frameworks like React.

Adopting the Agent Host Protocol in VS Code required building a new harness and agentic stack underneath it. The Copilot Chat extension, which used to provide all of VS Code’s AI and agentic capabilities, had been growing for over three years, reaching over 400,000 lines of TypeScript, not counting tests. But, as a VS Code extension, it was structured around the lifecycle of a VS Code window and depended entirely on the VS Code extension API. Neither that lifetime nor those APIs would be available in a standalone agent host process.

As we started building the Agent Host Protocol in early 2026, the Copilot SDK was also being developed, and it made sense to leverage it as the foundation for the new agentic stack. This was a big transition. It’s easy to build a harness implementation that says hello and can edit files—we were using the agent host to develop the agent host after the first couple weeks—but it’s another thing to build one that covers the long tail of features developers had come to expect from Copilot Chat.

GitHub has had Copilot cloud agents for a couple of years, allowing developers to run tasks in the cloud from any device. While VS Code was quietly developing the Agent Host Protocol, GitHub was looking to expand that capability to coordinate sessions running on your own devices via the Copilot app, or in cloud sandboxes. This was exactly the N:M problem that AHP was built to solve, so adopting the same technology made perfect sense.

From the outset, the GitHub Agent Host had different requirements than VS Code’s implementation. VS Code runs the host locally and can drop its host on other compute over SSH, containers, WSL, or Dev Tunnels, and it is actually included as part of the VS Code Server bundle. The GitHub Agent Host needed to run in the cloud and across a much wider variety of infrastructure, environments, and security constraints.

Those differing requirements are why they’re separate implementations written in different languages: VS Code’s in TypeScript and GitHub’s in Rust. For now, having two agent hosts has been a good acid-test for the protocol itself. How well does it work when two entirely different teams and sets of agents implement the same protocol independently?

Pretty well, it turns out. Like MCP, the Agent Host Protocol provides escape hatches via custom methods and _metadata on objects that consumers can use for out-of-band signaling. After several months heads-down on our respective implementations, our first task as we worked toward unification was inventorying the ways each implementation had extended the protocol. Many of these extensions reasonably stay GitHub-specific, such as the GitHub Agent Host using metadata to link to project boards. In other cases, variance was a sign of a gap in the protocol; early versions, for example, could not accurately represent shell-type tool output that wasn’t backed by a PTY. And some were bad assumptions, like VS Code’s client assuming a particular structure for the session URIs it received from the agent host. A protocol with only one implementation isn’t truly portable; as we taught our clients and servers to talk to each other, AHP became portable in practice, not just in theory.

Today, the Agent Host Protocol provides that same continuity across all VS Code and GitHub surfaces. Agent sessions running locally in the GitHub Copilot app, in cloud sandboxes, and in VS Code all share the same underlying protocol. To see how it’s used, read about how you can work from anywhere with the new connected Copilot cloud experience.

AHP was designed to be easy to use in clients and servers. Agent Host Protocol libraries are available in Rust, TypeScript, Kotlin, Swift, .NET, and Go, and agents are able to build clients and servers with little prompting. I have a macropad sitting on my desk that reflects my agent’s state in real time, and others have built everything from smart watch apps to Kanban-style task management tools against the protocol. Whether you want a companion app for your own agents, a way to bring agent sessions into your team’s tools, or AHP support in your own editor or agent, the protocol is open for you to build on.

You can connect to an agent host you implement in the Copilot app (enable Experimental > Direct Connections, then go to Environments > Add Agent Host Protocol Host) and in VS Code (via Add Remote Agent Host in the Agents window). You can spawn a standalone version of VS Code’s agent host by running code agent host in your terminal, or see the endpoints for the one in your editor by running code agent endpoints, and samples can help you and your agent get started.

The Agent Host Protocol is an open standard, and questions and suggestions are welcome on GitHub.

Explore the Agent Host Protocol on GitHub >

Agent skills capture repeatable engineering work in files teams can review and reuse, while tools, project context, and permissions remain specific to the host that runs them.

EXCLUSIVE: Kylie Jenner’s $38 Million Concrete Mansion Is Back on the Market With a Glamorous New Look

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Kylie Jenner has relisted her concrete fortress in Los Angeles for $38 million after giving the property a complete overhaul.

The reality TV personality, 29, initially put the seven-bedroom Holmby Hills property on the market on Dec. 22, according to property records, just over five years after she snapped it up for $36.5 million in 2020.

The extraordinary dwelling then lingered on the market for several months until June 24, when Realtor.com® exclusively revealed that the property had vanished from the selling block.

At the time, a source close to Jenner suggested to Realtor.com® that the property would be returning to the market. Then, in June, the listing came back with a very steep—and enticing—price cut as well as a sparkling new set of images and a fresh property description. Then, just last month, the home was once again removed from the market.

Now, the property has reappeared on the selling block with a fresh set of listing images—some of which have been polished by AI to show potential buyers what the property could look like.

The home, which is listed with Joshua Altman of Douglas Elliman of California, Inc., is described in its listing as "one of Los Angeles' most prestigious enclaves."

"Set in prime Holmby Hills and spanning approximately 15,227 square feet, this predominantly single-story estate was designed for those who demand it all: remarkable scale, fortress-level privacy, and an A-list lifestyle without ever leaving home," the listing description adds.

Just before entering the home, guests are greeted by 12-foot-tall gates that emphasize the importance of safety and privacy.

The living area comes complete with a cozy fireplace and an expansive view of the backyard, which features a resort-style pool.

The chef's kitchen is equipped with a marble island and seating area, plenty of cabinet space, and state-of-the-art appliances.

The spacious dining area is surrounded by all-white walls and topped by an elegant chandelier.

One of the many entertaining areas features a second bar and a pool table, making it the perfect place to welcome guests.

"Featuring 7 bedrooms, 14 bathrooms, a dedicated guard house, 4-car garage, and 5 additional covered parking spaces, this is the quintessential Holmby Hills trophy estate. A rare offering where grand-scale living, world-class amenities, and absolute discretion converge at one of Los Angeles' most coveted addresses," the listing reads.

Jenner may be trying to replicate the luck she had with her Hidden Hills mansion, which recently sold for $15.3 million.

Jenner, who is currently in a very high-profile relationship with actor Timothee Chalamet, purchased the sprawling 13,200-square-foot Hidden Hills dwelling for $12.05 million in late 2016, when she was just 19. It served as the on-screen star's primary residence for many years.

After listing the home for $20.25 million, Kylie sold it for $15.3 million, Realtor.com® exclusively revealed.

Jenner will not be quitting the Hidden Hills neighborhood—where the majority of her family members also reside—altogether, however. She purchased a huge plot of land in the area for $15 million in 2020, where she spent more than five years building her dream estate.

According to planning applications, Jenner had earmarked the parcel to serve as the location of an enormous 18,000-square-foot property that would offer her additional privacy and more room for her family to enjoy.

Among the features of her newly designed dwelling were a 12-car garage, a guesthouse, a sport court, and a security office, as well as an enormous swimming pool.

As of June 2025, images revealed that work on the project was nearly completed, suggesting that Jenner may have been preparing to move into her new abode full time.

In January, Jenner took her fans on a tour of the nearly completed property, showing off the different spaces and gushing about some of the design details that had been added, including the stone exteriors and the reclaimed wood floors.

On this day in space! Nov. 17, 1967: NASA's Surveyor 6 moon probe makes 1st-ever successful launch and hop from the lunar surface

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Surveyor 6 showed that spacecraft could land on the moon, and lift off again. The lives of NASA's Apollo astronauts depended on it.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

On Nov. 17, 1967, NASA's Surveyor 6 spacecraft made the first liftoff from the lunar surface.

Surveyor 6 was one of several robotic lunar missions NASA sent to scout the moon before sending humans there. It took television pictures of the surface and learned about the composition of lunar soil. Then came the big liftoff.

First Surveyor 6 launched from the surface and hopped to another position about 8 feet (2 meters) away. Next, Surveyor 6 took pictures of its original landing site. Flight controllers on Earth could see the landing pad prints of Surveyor 6. They also saw an imprint from the rocket exhaust when Surveyor 6 lifted off.

Surveyor 6 showed that spacecraft could land on the moon, and lift off again. Less than two years later, humans did the same thing during Apollo 11.

NASA's Surveyor 6 spacecraft launched to the moon on Nov. 7, 1967 and made its first landing on the lunar surface on Nov. 10 of that year. It's primary mission was to make a soft landing on the moon in the Sinus Medii of the moon.

The probe took nearly 30,000 photos of the moon during its 16 days on the lunar surface, with the lander shutting down due to the onset of lunar night on Nov. 26. Surveyor 6 was the sixth and penultimate moon mission of NASA's Surveyor lunar program, so a lot of what it did (launching and landing on the moon) had been done before.

But a lunar liftoff? That was something new.

"On November 17, as an engineering test to see if its rockets could be restarted on the Moon, Surveyor’s engines fired for 2.5 seconds, lifting the spacecraft 12 feet high and moving it 8 feet west of its original landing site, in the first powered liftoff from another celestial body," NASA wrote in a mission overview.

The engineering test proved that a spacecraft could land on the moon, and then lift off and take flight once more. That capability was a core requirement for NASA's Apollo astronaut moon landings that followed, since astronauts needed a way to return home after visiting the moon.

But there was critical science Surveyor 6 was able to do with the lunar hop.

"This maneuver provided excellent views of the surface disturbances produced by the initial landing and the effects of firing rocket engines close to the lunar surface," scientists with the Lunar and Planetary Institute wrote in a summary. "Photography obtained after the hop contributed to the soil mechanics investigation."

Surveyor 6 also had a bar magnet on its footpad that allowed scientists to test how magnetic the lunar regolith was at the landing site.

Want more space history? Check out our full On This Day In Space Story archive and watch our On This Day In Space videos on YouTube.

On this day in space! Nov. 16, 1974: The giant Arecibo Observatory in Puerto Rico broadcasts humanity's 1st interstellar message

After studying physics at the University of Tennessee in her hometown of Knoxville, she earned her graduate degree in Science, Health and Environmental Reporting (SHERP) from New York University. In her spare time, Hanneke enjoys exploring the Rocky Mountains, basking in nature and looking for dark skies to gaze at the cosmos.

MAI Code 2 and Code 2 Flash

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

MAI Code 2 and MAI Code 2 Flash: two new coding models built for the everyday enterprise coding tasks, delivering leading performance at a fraction of the cost of competing models.

MAI Code 2 is at the frontier of price-performance for common enterprise coding tasks and excels in taking code to production, deep debugging, multi-file implementation, repo wide reasoning, and UI. It does all of this while also delivering changes code maintainers would merge, at a fraction of frontier cost.

Meanwhile, MAI Code 2 Flash is designed for high-volume work such as code explanations, targeted edits, routine fixes, and rapid iterations, while bringing quality at a much lower cost.

To deliver the best price-performance, we’ve built these models to prioritize the coding work our customers do every day. They’re explicitly not yet tuned for harder problems, like fundamental science research and very long horizon agentic work.

Averaged over four widely used benchmarks that target the everyday enterprise coding tasks we see in GitHub Copilot (DeepSWE 1.1, Terminal Bench 2.1, SWE-Atlas and FrontierCode v1.1), MAI Code 2 gives you 98% of the performance of GPT 6.1 Sol at 40% of the cost, and 96% of Claude Opus 5.5’s performance at just 5% of the cost.1

MAI Code 2 is also tuned for producing great dashboards and visualizations, and excels at visual coding. In blind frontend app evaluations, reviewers preferred its results over GPT-6 Sol 67% of the time.2

In addition, MAI Code 2 Flash offers comparable performance to GPT-6 Luna and Claude Haiku 5.5 at 65% and 20% of the cost, across three coding benchmarks measuring day-to-day, high-volume routine coding tasks.3

MAI Code 2 is also becoming the workhorse behind Microsoft products. From Code 2 Flash we further optimized a single model, MAI Work Flash, to serve multiple Microsoft products across M365 and Fabric IQ.

Excel: Our model is on par for accuracy and token efficiency with GPT 6 Luna across key internal Excel customer-scenario benchmarks.

PowerPoint: On key production benchmark scenarios that measure frequent PowerPoint tasks, our model performs on par with GPT 6 Luna in both quality and token efficiency.

Word: Our model performs similar to GPT 6.1 sol (while being significantly smaller) on 5 key internal benchmarks using ~42% fewer input tokens per call than GPT6.1 Sol in production.

Teams: Our model beats GPT 6 Luna at tracking decisions, filling knowledge gaps, and adapting custom instructions using up to 28% fewer tokens on instruction following and tool calling.

FabricIQ: In offline evaluation runs our MAI model outperforms GPT-5.6-sol, GPT-6-astra, and Opus-5.5 at DAX query generation, the core capability behind “Chat with your Data” for Power BI semantic models.

MAI Code 2 and Code 2 Flash are available in GitHub Copilot, and Code 2 Flash is in limited-access preview in Microsoft Foundry. Soon, we’ll be releasing it on Open Router, Vercel, Hermes, Open Code, and Kilo.

Learn more about customizing MAI Code 2 on our Custom Models page.

About these results Results reflect evaluations on specific coding tasks and configurations. Performance and cost comparisons vary by workload, model configuration, reasoning effort, and pricing assumptions.

OpenAI sued by California after admitting to 80 loss-of-control incidents

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

AG: OpenAI tested dangerous AI without proper oversight and misled public about it.

California sued OpenAI yesterday, alleging it has tried to evade responsibility for the Hugging Face attack and about 80 other incidents in which the company lost control of its AI. The lawsuit was filed under the state’s public nuisance and unfair competition laws.

The July 2026 hack of Hugging Face and other attacks carried out by OpenAI’s autonomous agents “may just be the tip of the iceberg,” said a lawsuit filed in a state court by California Attorney General Rob Bonta. “OpenAI recently admitted to the Attorney General’s Office that it has lost control of its autonomous AI agents over 80 times during testing.”

OpenAI had publicly reported only 27 such incidents, showing that “OpenAI’s public reports do not represent the full scope of loss-of-control incidents involving OpenAI’s models that have impacted third parties,” the lawsuit said. OpenAI “knowingly tested a dangerous product without proper oversight or safety procedures and misled the public about it,” Bonta said in a press release.

OpenAI models are “able to find novel vulnerabilities in computer networks, quickly break down defenses, and autonomously carry out cyberattacks,” Bonta’s lawsuit said. OpenAI puts safeguards in its publicly released models to prevent this behavior, “but deliberately omits or removes these protections while experimenting with and training its models,” the lawsuit said.

In the attack on Hugging Face, OpenAI agents “employed complicated hacking methodologies and involved coordinated actions by hundreds of individual autonomous AI agents that should have been contained in OpenAI’s research environment,” the lawsuit said.

Known attacks “include unauthorized access of and cyberattacks on databases containing code that powers countless applications; informational websites that are trusted and valuable resources for the public; and foreign government databases storing sensitive health information,” the lawsuit said. “Swarms of OpenAI’s experimental AI agents have even compromised OpenAI’s own internal infrastructure, in an attack that managed to obtain administrator-level access to critical OpenAI systems.”

The lawsuit accused OpenAI of refusing to provide sufficient detail about the incidents. Bonta announced an investigation into OpenAI in early September and sent an investigative subpoena to the company with additional questions on September 30.

“Rather than take responsibility and be transparent with the public and with regulators so that these loss-of-control incidents can be analyzed and prevented, OpenAI thwarted the Attorney General’s efforts to investigate these incidents, including by refusing to provide detailed information about the full universe of incidents known to the company,” the lawsuit filed yesterday said.

OpenAI told Ars that most of the incidents identified in its review had low severity, and none matched the severity or scale of the Hugging Face attack. OpenAI defended its response to the California investigation, saying it provided multiple technical briefings to Bonta and his staff, answered the office’s questions, and offered more technical briefings to discuss its remediation efforts and plans for addressing the problems.

“Since the Hugging Face incident, we’ve undertaken an extensive and transparent effort to understand and address misaligned model activity,” OpenAI said. “We’ve published a detailed technical report, commissioned and published a separate independent report, notified affected organizations, strengthened safeguards across our research systems, and created a framework for additional identification and disclosure of misalignment. This lawsuit appears to single out OpenAI over a challenge that’s been identified by every major AI lab. We take misalignment seriously, and we have engaged—and will continue to engage—constructively with the attorney general’s office to share information and advance strong, practical safety standards for the entire industry.”

California’s lawsuit accuses OpenAI of creating a public nuisance as defined in state law, and several violations of the state’s Unfair Competition Law. Alleged violations include “causing and/or allowing agents to gain unauthorized access to third-party computer systems,” developing AI models without adequate security measures, and making false or misleading claims about the safety of its AI products.

California is seeking an injunction to stop the alleged violations of the Unfair Competition Law and to require civil penalties and financial damages. It also wants an order to “compel OpenAI to abate the ongoing public nuisance their conduct has created in California.” Bonta said in his press release that “the Hugging Face attack was the predictable result of an internal culture at OpenAI that prioritizes growth and rapid model development over safety and accountability—and this was only one of 80 known incidents.”

OpenAI was sued in September by Legal Advocates for Safe Science & Technology (LASST), a nonprofit that alleged violations of California’s Unfair Competition Law and the state’s Comprehensive Computer Data Access and Fraud Act. “OpenAI and frontier AI developers more broadly can’t avoid the consequences of their unsafe actions just by claiming that ‘an AI did it,’” the group said. “Autonomous AI agents will continue to hack, steal data, disrupt systems, and violate rights until a court steps in.”

OpenAI said in response to the LASST lawsuit that “Hugging Face was a serious incident and we’ve taken a series of actions in response to it, but this lawsuit is completely without merit.”

After all, you don’t need to know everything, only what’s important.

Firaxis reportedly cuts a third of its staff as studio head departs and new XCOM struggles

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

Civilization and XCOM developer Firaxis Games has laid off around 60 people.

The cuts, which took place today, account for about a third of the studio’s staff, Bloomberg reports, citing people familiar with the matter. Studio head Heather Hazen is also leaving.

Its sources say Firaxis' unannounced XCOM game has faced development struggles and missed "a significant milestone over the summer". The project will continue, but "may face a shakeup as a result of the cuts".

A spokesperson for 2K, the Take-Two label that runs Firaxis, confirmed Hazen's departure to Bloomberg, and said the studio is "restructuring and recentering around the studio's core strength - designing world class strategy games that have set the industry standard for the genre for the last 30 years".

"As part of these changes, the size of the studio is being reduced to focus on design, technology and the player experience, which means saying goodbye to talented colleagues who helped build Firaxis," the spokesperson continued. "These decisions are never easy, but they’re essential to build the next chapter of Firaxis."

The layoffs come two days before the launch of Grand Theft Auto 6, which is developed and published by Rockstar, a separate label at Take-Two.

In September, around 18 months after Civ 7's widely criticised launch - and as the legendary 4X series celebrates its 35th anniversary - Firaxis unveiled a host of new updates coming to the game as it continued to improve it. These included an atomic age update, dragging Civ 7 into the present and beyond, as well as its first paid expansion, Earthrise, both arriving next year.

Take-Two boss Strauss Zelnick admitted earlier this year that the studio "got it wrong with Civ 7".

"The game is a really good game. And it's certainly a profitable enterprise for us. But this is one where I think what we tried to do was a bridge too far, from the consumer's perspective," he told Game File in May.

It’s not the first round of cuts at the studio - 2K confirmed an unspecified number of layoffs at Firaxis in September 2025, and around 30 jobs went in 2023.

Clouds block satellite cameras, so an AI learned how to redraw the ground beneath them

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

GeoCore-9B learned how to recreate the ground that is hidden beneath cloud cover in satellite photos.

A satellite picture of a flooded town is only worth something if the camera can see the town. On a cloudy day it can’t, and the picture shows cloud where the streets should be. A new AI model fills those streets back in.

It was built to know where on Earth it is looking and how much ground each pixel covers.

Jeonghyeok Do and Munchurl Kim of KAIST in South Korea built the model, called GeoCore-9B. Their paper is a preprint that has been accepted for presentation at NeurIPS 2026, an artificial intelligence conference. It has not yet been published in a journal.

The ground the model draws under a cloud is a reconstruction, not a photograph. Do and Kim make that point themselves, and they warn that realistic fake satellite imagery could be used to spread disinformation about disasters or geopolitical events.

Ordinary satellite pictures work like a camera. They record sunlight bouncing off the ground, so thick cloud or nighttime leaves them with nothing to record.

That matters most during floods and wildfires, when the weather is often bad and the ground is exactly what people need to see.

A radar satellite sends radio waves down and measures the echoes that come back. The method is called synthetic aperture radar, and it works at night and through cloud.

But its images don’t look like photographs, because brightness and texture in them mean different things. Do and Kim wanted one model for both problems.

Earlier attempts started with an AI built to generate ordinary photographs and then taught it about satellites. Do and Kim wrote that this builds in biases.

Ordinary photos are taken from eye level with a subject in the middle, while a satellite looks straight down at a scene with no center.

So they trained their AI model from scratch on satellite views alone, adjusting about 9 billion internal settings, known as parameters, as it learned.

The training set was 10 million satellite images, each paired with a sentence describing it. The note for a port mentioned ships and warehouses; the note for farmland mentioned a road between fields.

Each image also carried its latitude, longitude, and how much ground one pixel covers. A building looks very different when each pixel covers about 3 feet (1 meter) than when each covers about 105 feet (32 meters).

Without that number, a small building and a large industrial plant can have the same shape.

A model this large, trained from nothing, learns slowly and tends to produce scenes with no coherent layout. So Do and Kim brought in a second AI, already trained to recognize satellite imagery, as a teacher.

During training, the new model was scored on how well its internal picture of a scene agreed with the teacher’s picture of how roads connect and where field boundaries lie.

The teacher is switched off once training ends, so it adds no work when the model is in use. Without it, the team found, the model’s images had fragmented textures and distorted structures. With it, every score they tracked improved.

The team also tested what the model had absorbed about geography. Given only a latitude and longitude, with no text at all, it drew dense skyscrapers for the coordinates of New York City and ice fields for Antarctica. It drew sand dunes for the Sahara and forest for the Amazon.

The team checked 500 of these coordinate-only images generated by the model against all 10.5 million real world photos that were used as training images.

They found none that closely matched the real-world satellite images, suggesting that the AI was not simply copying existing photographs. However, they wrote that the test can’t rule out every form of memorization used by the AI model.

To put the model to work, Do and Kim froze it and trained a small set of extra settings for each task. This was far cheaper than retraining it.

On a public test set of cloudy and clear image pairs, GeoCore-9B outscored every program built specifically for cloud removal on two of three standard measures. Those were cover brightness and color accuracy, and how closely the structure matches the clear image.

On the third, how natural the image looks, it scored about the same as the best specialist. The roads and field boundaries in its reconstructions stayed where the clear images put them.

On a second public dataset, the model turned radar images into photo-like images. It scored best on the two measures of realism and fine detail, and it roughly matched the best specialist on structural similarity.

“By training the AI on where each satellite image was taken and the actual size of the terrain it shows, we made it possible to use a single model for multiple tasks such as cloud removal and image conversion,” said Kim, a professor in KAIST’s School of Electrical Engineering.

He added that he expects the work to make weather-limited satellite data more usable for assessing disasters and monitoring changes in forests and cities.

The model only produces ordinary color images. It can read a radar image but can’t generate one. In addition, it doesn’t handle the multispectral images, with light bands beyond visible color, that some Earth-observation satellites record.

One piece, the component that compresses each image before the model works on it, came from ordinary photography. On the cloud-removal images, it kept the main features but lost some faint local detail.

By their own account, the experiments can’t say how much of the gain came from the model’s size, its training data, or the teacher.

So far each task has been tested on a single public dataset. How the model performs on other satellites and regions is unknown, and that is the test Do and Kim plan next.

Do and Kim also want the model to predict changes on the ground: flooding, building damage, wildfire spread, and urban growth. The team plans to explore surveillance and reconnaissance as well.

The model’s code and weights are online, so other groups can run those tests.

The full study has been posted as a preprint on arXiv and accepted for presentation at NeurIPS 2026. It has not been published in a journal.

Voyager 1 reaches 1 light-day from Earth

📅 Nov 17, 2026  |  Category: Technology  |  Read Source →

At 5:16 a.m. EST on Wednesday, Nov. 18, Voyager 1 will become the first spacecraft to reach a distance of 1 light-day from Earth. A command radioed from home will need 24 hours to arrive, and a reply another 24 to come back. NASA is asking the public to celebrate by sending the spacecraft a signal on the evening of Tuesday, Nov. 17.

NASA launched the twin Voyager spacecraft in 1977 to tour the giant planets: Jupiter, Saturn, Uranus, and Neptune. Voyager 1 flew past Jupiter and Saturn, while Voyager 2 went on to Uranus and Neptune and is still the only spacecraft to visit either one. Both then kept going. They’re now the only probes ever to study interstellar space directly, the region beyond the Sun’s magnetic field.

Each spacecraft runs on a radioisotope thermoelectric generator, the same kind of power source that drives NASA’s Curiosity and Perseverance rovers on Mars. Plutonium-238, the fuel source, gives off heat as it naturally decays, and the generators turn that heat into electricity. But because the output fades by about 4 watts every year, engineers have spent years shutting off components and instruments to stretch the spacecraft’s lives. This year, a risky maneuver nicknamed the “Big Bang” turned off several devices in one coordinated move to buy the spacecraft and their remaining instruments several more years of operation. Today, Voyager 1 only runs two of its original 10 instruments, and Voyager 2 runs three.

Voyager 1 is closing in on 1 light-day from Earth (about 16.1 billion miles [25.9 billion kilometers]) — farther than any spacecraft has ever gone. Its twin is about 2 billion miles (3.2 billion km) closer to Earth, near enough that a signal takes about 20 hours to reach Voyager 2.

Voyager 1 lifted off Sept. 5, 1977, two weeks after Voyager 2 but on a shorter trajectory as it was traveling to Jupiter. After visiting Saturn in 1980, it drifted away from the solar system’s planets for good. A decade later, it captured the famous photograph of Earth known as the Pale Blue Dot. In August 2012, it became the first spacecraft to enter interstellar space, and Voyager 2 followed in 2018. Voyager 2 won’t reach the light-day mark until November 2035. You can see both missions’ exact locations on NASA’s mission status page.

A light-day is a distance measuring how far light travels in 24 hours. At about 186,000 miles per second (299,792 km per second), that adds up to roughly 16.1 billion miles (25.9 billion km). For scale, the Moon is just 1.3 light-seconds away, the Sun about 8.3 light-minutes, and Neptune a bit over 4 light-hours. Proxima Centauri, the nearest star beyond the Sun, lies more than 1,500 light-days away.

At a speed of nearly 38,000 mph (61,000 km/h), Voyager 1 is anything but slow. Still, it needed more than 49 years to cover the distance light crosses in a single day.

Astronomy usually asks readers to dim their lights. But tomorrow night is an exception: To celebrate Voyager 1’s accomplishment, NASA invites everyone to flash Voyager’s call sign, V-G-R, skyward in Morse code on Tuesday evening. Use NASA’s automated flasher on your smartphone or tablet. Or grab a plain flashlight and do it manually: V: dot-dot-dot-dash; G: dash-dash-dot; R: dot-dash-dot. Dots are quick blinks of the light, and dashes are long flashes of about a second. Pause between letters.

Brooks Mendenhall is a staff writer at Astronomy, based in Chattanooga, Tennessee, fueled by an unending curiosity about the universe. A former classroom teacher, he has a knack for breaking down complex concepts for a wide audience.
